Acknowledgement
We would like to express our deepest gratitude to Dr. Hassan Ibrahim for his invaluable guidance, continuous support, and expert supervision throughout the development of this Swarm Robot System project. His technical insights and constructive feedback have been instrumental in shaping the direction and quality of this research.
We extend our sincere thanks to the Department of Computer Engineering at May University in Cairo for providing us with the necessary resources and support required for the successful completion of this project.
We also acknowledge the challenges we faced as Computer Engineering students learning new domains such as electronics, mechanical design, and robotics hardware. The support from our colleagues and the broader robotics community through online resources and documentation was invaluable.
Finally, we would like to thank our families for their encouragement and understanding throughout the duration of this demanding academic endeavor.

Abstract
The Swarm Robot System represents an approach to autonomous collaborative robotics, designed for environments such as search and rescue operations. This project presents a framework for coordinating multiple autonomous robots working together to accomplish tasks.The system architecture is built upon many cooperative robots’ platforms that work together to perform all required functions. The Primary Robot handles LIDAR-based SLAM for environmental mapping and hosts the camera for object detection using YOLO. The Secondary Robot provides support functions and additional coverage.A key aspect of our approach is the implementation of a coordinated architecture where both robots share a common map representation. The software framework is built on the Robot Operating System (ROS), and communication between robots uses TCP protocol over a 5GHz WiFi network.This project also represents significant learning for the team, as Computer Engineering students with limited prior exposure to electronics, mechanical design, and robotics hardware. The team divided responsibilities into programming, design, electronics, and environment preparation, while maintaining cross-functional understanding.The system currently supports autonomous mapping and object detection within the mapped environment. The testing environment is a 4x4 meter area divided into four rooms.
Keywords: Swarm Robotics, Multi-Robot Systems, SLAM, Sensor Fusion, Object Detection, Search and Rescue, ROS, YOLO

Chapter 1

Chapter 1: Introduction
1.1 Project Overview
The Swarm Robot System project represents an advancement in multi-robot autonomous systems design, specifically targeting collaborative robotics for environmental exploration and hazard assessment. This project presents a framework for coordinating multiple autonomous robots working together to accomplish tasks that would be difficult or dangerous for a single robotic unit<w:id w:val="-1053385507"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/><w:sz w:val="24"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1]<w:id w:val="172458591"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/><w:sz w:val="24"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2].
The fundamental concept underlying this project is the principle of "Collaborative Intelligence" – distributing computational and sensory tasks across multiple specialized robotic units, each contributing specific functions while working in coordination. Our system deploys two complementary robots that together handle all required functions: environmental mapping using SLAM technology, object and human detection using deep learning, and environmental monitoring<w:id w:val="1545876083"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/><w:sz w:val="24"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1], [3].
Project Context and Significance
In contemporary applications such as search and rescue operations, disaster assessment, and structural inspection, the deployment of human personnel is often dangerous and time-consuming. The Swarm Robot System addresses these challenges by providing an autonomous platform that can systematically explore unknown environments, identify hazards, locate objects of interest, and generate actionable intelligence in real-time.
The innovation in our approach lies in architecting an integrated system where each robot's capabilities are purposefully designed, and where data from all robots feeds into a shared, unified representation of the environment. This shared map becomes the foundation upon which all robots make decisions, enabling coordinated behavior<w:id w:val="-1160298887"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/><w:sz w:val="24"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4]<w:id w:val="-1483532462"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/><w:sz w:val="24"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1].
System Composition
The system comprises three autonomous mobile robots that work together to perform all required functions:
Primary Robot (Mapping and Navigation): Equipped with LIDAR, IMU, wheel encoders, camera, and advanced onboard processing using Raspberry Pi 4 (8GB RAM). This robot performs continuous SLAM to generate precise 2D environmental maps and hosts the camera for object detection<w:id w:val="-1016925646"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4].
Secondary Robot (Support and Communication): Equipped with Raspberry Pi 3B+, IMU, wheel encoders, and additional sensors. This robot supports the primary robot in coverage and provides redundancy in the system<w:id w:val="-1130007733"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5].
Environmental Monitoring Robot (Gas Detection): Equipped with an ESP32 module for network communication, an Arduino microcontroller, wheel encoders, ultrasonic sensors, and a gas sensor. This robot is responsible for detecting hazardous gases and monitoring environmental conditions, while transmitting sensor data to the system in real time to support safety and situational awareness <w:id w:val="-261604412"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="296426A0F35C4231B5B3D812BEC7A08E"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5].
All robots communicate through a 5GHz WiFi network using TCP protocol, ensuring reliable data transmission between the robots and the central dashboard for monitoring and control<w:id w:val="483974323"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
Technical Approach
Key technical aspects of this project include:
Shared Map Architecture: The Primary Robot generates a map using LIDAR-based SLAM, which is then shared across the system via ROS map server<w:id w:val="414528596"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5].
Sensor Fusion: Extended Kalman Filters integrate odometry data from wheel encoders with IMU readings for precise localization
Deep Learning Integration: YOLO-based object detection for identifying humans and objects within the environment
Real-time Dashboard: Web-based monitoring interface for operators to view robot status, maps, and detections<w:id w:val="521144378"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
1.2 Background and Context
Project Background and Prior Research
Before beginning the implementation of this project, extensive research was conducted to understand existing work in the field of multi-robot systems, SLAM technology, and search and rescue robotics. This research phase was critical as the team members, being Computer Engineering students, had limited prior exposure to robotics hardware, electronics, and mechanical design<w:id w:val="1163591919"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[7].
The research phase involved reviewing numerous academic papers, open-source projects, and existing implementations of similar systems. Key areas of study included:
Previous implementations of mapping robots and SLAM algorithms
Multi-robot coordination frameworks and communication protocols
Object detection systems using deep learning
ROS (Robot Operating System) architecture and packages
Sensor integration techniques for mobile robots
This review of prior work provided the foundation for understanding what components would be needed, what challenges to expect, and what approaches had been successful in similar projects<w:id w:val="-12151403"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[8].
Evolution of Robotic Systems
The field of robotics has undergone significant transformation over the past two decades. Early robotic systems were primarily stationary, task-specific machines designed for manufacturing environments. The emergence of mobile robotics introduced new challenges: navigation in unstructured environments, real-time decision making, sensor uncertainty management, and power-constrained operation<w:id w:val="-2051908969"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1].
The evolution toward multi-robot systems represents the next step in robotics. Rather than solving increasingly complex problems with increasingly complex individual robots, the research community has recognized the advantages of distributed systems: redundancy, parallelization of tasks, natural scalability, and fault tolerance.
Search and Rescue Applications
Search and rescue (SAR) operations represent one of the most compelling use cases for autonomous robotic systems. Traditional SAR operations are characterized by:
Environmental Hazards: Unstable structures, toxic atmospheres, extreme temperatures
Information Scarcity: Lack of prior maps or detailed environmental knowledge
Time Pressure: Every hour of delay reduces survival probability
Resource Constraints: Limited availability of trained personnel and specialized equipment
Robotic systems can address these challenges by providing rapid, systematic environmental assessment without exposing human personnel to hazards<w:id w:val="-1725984561"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1], [4].
A summary of the major application domains of swarm robotic systems, along with representative tasks and their advantages, is presented in Table 1.1.

SLAM Technology
Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM) has evolved from a theoretical concept to a practical, reliable technology. Key developments in SLAM include particle filter-based approaches (FastSLAM), visual SLAM systems (ORB-SLAM), and integration with deep learning. Our implementation leverages GMapping, a well-established ROS-integrated SLAM library<w:id w:val="1487045983"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5],<w:id w:val="1983888155"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[9].
Deep Learning for Object Detection
The integration of deep learning with robotic systems has transformed robot perception. Object detection systems such as YOLO enable robots to recognize objects and humans with high accuracy. Our project uses YOLO for human and object detection within the mapped environment<w:id w:val="304754496"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[10],<w:id w:val="-1255202289"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[11].
Robot Operating System (ROS)
ROS has emerged as the standard platform for robotics research and development. ROS provides:
Standardized Middleware: Publish-subscribe message passing and service calls.
Sensor Integration Libraries: Support for various sensors via dedicated drivers.
Algorithm Libraries: Implementations of SLAM, motion planning, and control algorithms.
Simulation Environment: Gazebo simulator for testing without physical robots.
Community and Ecosystem: Extensive documentation and pre-built components<w:id w:val="476121082"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
Learning Challenges
As Computer Engineering students, the team faced significant learning challenges during this project. Prior to this project, the team had limited exposure to:
Electronics and circuit design
Robotics hardware and mechanical systems
3D design and CAD software (such as Inventor)
Metal fabrication and chassis construction
This required substantial time investment in learning these skills from the ground up, which added to the complexity and timeline of the project. Each team member working on a specific domain still maintained general understanding of other parts of the project to ensure effective collaboration.
1.3 Problem Statement
The Challenge of Autonomous Multi-Robot Systems
Despite significant advances in individual robot capabilities, deploying multiple robots to work collaboratively in unknown environments presents several challenges<w:id w:val="1647621829"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:eastAsia="MS Mincho" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/><w:szCs w:val="24"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2]:
Challenge 1: Environmental Representation
Problem: Different robots need to work within the same physical environment. Without a shared representation of that environment (a map), robots cannot coordinate movements or target specific locations. Traditional approaches require each robot to independently map its environment, which creates consistency problems when different robots observe the same spac.
Impact: Redundant computation, lack of true coordination, potential conflicts when different robots' maps disagree about environmental features.
Challenge 2: Localization Without Global Reference
Problem: Most autonomous systems rely on GPS for localization. GPS is unavailable indoors, underground, or in urban environments – precisely the environments where search and rescue operations occur. Without GPS, robots must localize themselves using only onboard sensors. Traditional odometry accumulates error over distance, making it unreliable for larger environments.
Impact: Robots cannot reliably navigate, cannot return to locations of interest, and task completion becomes unreliable.
Challenge 3: Data Processing in Real-Time
Problem: Modern object detection systems require significant computational resources that exceed the capabilities of low-power embedded systems. Running these algorithms on a Raspberry Pi results in slow performance. Transmitting raw video streams from robots to an external processing station creates bandwidth demands.
Impact: Slow response times, inefficient bandwidth utilization, potential communication delays.
Challenge 4: System Reliability
Problem: Any component failure (sensor malfunction, processor crash, network connectivity loss) can affect the system if not properly handled. Operating robots in challenging environments means component issues are likely.
Impact: Potential mission interruption, need for robust error handling.
Challenge 5: Real-Time Coordination
Problem: Coordinating multiple robots requires reliable communication and data synchronization. Network latency and message loss can affect coordination. The challenge is finding the right balance between centralized monitoring and distributed operation.
Impact: Coordination difficulties, vulnerability to communication issues.
Combined Problem Formulation
The overarching problem addressed by this project is: How can we design a reliable multi-robot system capable of autonomously exploring unknown environments and generating actionable intelligence through coordinated sensor deployment and information sharing?
This problem encompasses:
Environmental representation and map sharing
Reliable localization in GPS-denied environments
Real-time distributed intelligence
System reliability and communication
Practical implementation using available components
1.4 Motivation
Operational Motivations
The motivation for this project originates from real-world operational needs:
Search and Rescue Operations: When buildings collapse, become flooded, or are exposed to dangerous conditions, rescue teams face difficult choices. Robotic systems can compress the timeline of initial assessment. Our system specifically addresses the need for rapid environmental mapping combined with search for objects of interest and identification of hazards.
Educational Motivation: For Computer Engineering students, this project provides hands-on experience across multiple domains: embedded systems programming, distributed computing, artificial intelligence, sensor systems, and systems engineering. It bridges the gap between theoretical concepts taught in university courses and practical implementation challenges.
Research Contribution: While SLAM, sensor fusion, and object detection are individually well-studied, their integration into a practical multi-robot platform requires novel engineering work. Our approach of coordinating two robots with shared mapping and distributed sensing demonstrates practical multi-robot coordination<w:id w:val="334894490"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1]<w:id w:val="117419805"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2].
Technical Motivations
Sensor Fusion: The integration of Extended Kalman Filters with wheel encoder and IMU data demonstrates the power of multi-sensor integration. This technique improves localization accuracy compared to using odometry alone.
Edge Computing: Deploying deep learning inference while maintaining responsiveness on low-power robots addresses a practical challenge in robotics: how to execute computationally complex algorithms within power and latency constraints.
ROS Standardization: Building the system within the ROS ecosystem ensures that results are reproducible and extensible. Each component (SLAM, object detection, communication) is implemented as modular ROS packages<w:id w:val="-1155366997"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[12],<w:id w:val="-1323421555"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[13].
Learning Motivation
A significant motivation for this project was the opportunity to learn new skills outside the traditional Computer Engineering curriculum:
Programming: Learning ROS, YOLO, and robotics-specific programming paradigms.

1.5 Project Objectives
Primary Objectives
The project is organized around clearly defined objectives<w:id w:val="323100481"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6]:
Objective 1: Design and Implementation of Robot Platforms
Develop two robot platforms that work together to perform all required functions:
Primary Robot: SLAM capability, environmental mapping, camera integration, object detection
Secondary Robot: Support functions, communication, additional coverage
Environmental Monitoring Robot: Gas detection, environmental sensing, hazard monitoring
Success Criteria:
Robots achieve autonomous navigation in structured indoor environments
Robots can generate environmental maps using SLAM
Object detection identifies humans and common objects
Gas sensor detects hazardous gases and provides real-time environmental data
Objective 2: Shared Map Architecture
Implement a map representation that enables coordination:
Develop mechanisms for generating environmental maps from LIDAR data
Implement ROS map_server for sharing maps
Validate that robots can localize on shared maps
Objective 3: Sensor Fusion and Localization
Implement Extended Kalman Filters that fuse odometry and IMU data for accurate localization:
Fusion algorithm improves accuracy compared to odometry alone
System maintains localization during continuous operation
Objective 4: Object Detection and Classification
Integrate deep learning models for identifying objects and humans:

Assumptions
The project makes the following assumptions:
WiFi network (5GHz) will be available in the operating area
Operating environment is structured indoor space
Environment is relatively static during operation
Operator is available to monitor the system

1.9 Organization of the Dissertation
This dissertation is organized into the following chapters:
Chapter 1: Introduction
Establishes the context, motivation, and objectives of the project. Provides background on multi-robot systems and defines the problem statement, scope, and constraints.
Chapter 2: Literature Review and Related Work
Surveys existing research and systems in relevant domains: swarm robotics, SLAM, object detection, and communication frameworks. Reviews prior work that informed this project's design.
Chapter 3: System Analysis and Design
Details the system architecture, design process, and specification of hardware and software components.
Chapter 4: Implementation
Documents the practical realization of the design, including hardware assembly, software implementation, and integration.
Chapter 5: Testing and Results
Presents testing approach and results validating system functionality.
Chapter 6: Conclusion and Future Work
Summarizes achievements and outlines potential future enhancements.









CHAPTER 2

CHAPTER 2: Literature Review and Related Work
2.1 Introduction to Swarm and Cooperative Robotics
This chapter presents a comprehensive review of existing research, projects, and systems related to the Swarm Robot System. Before beginning the implementation phase of this project, extensive research was conducted to understand the current state of the art in multi-robot systems, SLAM technology, object detection, and related fields<w:id w:val="1392151574"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2].
The literature review served several important purposes:
Understanding existing approaches and their strengths and limitations
Identifying suitable technologies and tools for our implementation
Learning from previous implementations and avoiding common pitfalls
Determining the components and architecture needed for our system
Establishing realistic expectations for system capabilities
With our limited prior exposure to robotics hardware and mechanical systems, this research phase was particularly critical. We needed to understand not just the theoretical concepts but also the practical implementation details of similar projects.
Various coordination strategies have been proposed in the literature to enable collective behavior in swarm robotic systems. These strategies are classified in Table 2.1.
Table 2.1: Classification of swarm robotics coordination strategies

2.2 Review of Multi-Robot and Swarm Systems
Swarm robotics represents a research paradigm in which multiple robots cooperate to achieve common objectives through decentralized control and local interactions. This approach is inspired by natural systems such as ant colonies, bee swarms, and bird flocks, where complex collective behavior emerges from relatively simple individual agents following basic rules.
Unlike traditional single-robot systems, multi-robot and swarm systems emphasize collaboration, redundancy, and adaptability. Through the conducted literature review, several fundamental characteristics were identified as essential for the success of multi-robot systems<w:id w:val="2134060850"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1]:
Coordination: Robots must coordinate their actions to avoid conflicts, share tasks, and achieve collective goals efficiently. Coordination mechanisms may be centralized, decentralized, or hybrid, depending on system scale and application requirements <w:id w:val="1286238875"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="7B384A02E1144CABBB7613CC70EB9688"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4],<w:id w:val="1715771579"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="7B384A02E1144CABBB7613CC70EB9688"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[14].
Communication: Reliable communication is a core requirement, enabling robots to exchange state information, sensor data, and task assignments. Communication limitations directly affect coordination quality and system performance <w:id w:val="-1997256101"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="22AE6A2D8EA54930AA0E5AB3F884344A"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4],<w:id w:val="-781340954"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="22AE6A2D8EA54930AA0E5AB3F884344A"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[14].
Scalability: A well-designed swarm system should function effectively with varying numbers of robots. Scalability ensures that adding or removing robots does not significantly degrade system performance <w:id w:val="-1533494759"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="7355ACDADD254AFD90B0044DC07B599E"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4],<w:id w:val="-1054538984"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="7355ACDADD254AFD90B0044DC07B599E"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[14].
Robustness: Multi-robot systems must tolerate individual robot failures without complete system breakdown. Redundancy and distributed control increase resilience in dynamic or hazardous environments <w:id w:val="1108235227"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="28C87BD4A4A24736874F82B4212A0733"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4],<w:id w:val="1382208305"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="28C87BD4A4A24736874F82B4212A0733"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[14].
These principles guided the analysis of existing systems and influenced the design decisions adopted in this project.
2.2.1 Fundamental Concepts
Swarm robotics is a research paradigm in which multiple robots cooperate to accomplish shared tasks through distributed control and local interactions. The concept is inspired by biological systems such as ant colonies and bee swarms, where complex group behavior emerges from simple rules followed by individual agents rather than centralized intelligence<w:id w:val="-1819257977"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2].
Through the literature review conducted in this project, several core principles were identified as essential for the design of effective multi-robot systems. These principles directly influenced the architectural and implementation decisions adopted in our project.
Coordination: Robots must coordinate their actions to avoid conflicts and efficiently achieve shared objectives. In our project, this concept motivated the adoption of a master-centered design, where one robot currently performs mapping and decision-making, with the structure prepared for future multi-robot coordination.
Communication: Reliable communication is critical for information sharing between robots. This requirement influenced our focus on designing a stable communication architecture capable of supporting data exchange between robots and external processing units.
Scalability: Multi-robot systems should maintain functionality as the number of robots increases. This concept guided our use of modular software components and ROS-based design, allowing future extension without major system redesign.
Robustness: A swarm system should continue functioning even if individual robots fail. Although full redundancy is not yet implemented, this principle informed our decision to separate sensing, perception, and control into independent modules to minimize single points of failure.
Several path planning algorithms have been proposed for multi-robot navigation in dynamic environments. A comparison of common approaches is provided in Table 2.2.

2.2.2 Reviewed Projects and Systems
Several existing multi-robot and swarm systems were reviewed to understand both theoretical Several academic, commercial, and open-source systems were reviewed to understand how multi-robot concepts are applied in real-world scenarios and how they can inform system design.
Academic Swarm Systems
The Kilobot Swarm project developed at MIT demonstrated that large numbers of simple robots can collectively perform complex tasks through local communication and minimal sensing. This work showed that individual robots do not need advanced hardware to contribute meaningfully to group behavior. The main insight applied to our project is the emphasis on simple, modular robot design supported by effective communication strategies<w:id w:val="-627467314"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2].
Research and Educational Platforms
The TurtleBot series was reviewed as a representative example of widely adopted mobile robot platforms. TurtleBot implementations demonstrate the effectiveness of ROS-based software architectures, differential-drive locomotion, and LiDAR-based SLAM for indoor environments. These findings directly influenced our project’s decision to adopt ROS, use a differential-drive configuration, and rely on 2D LiDAR for mapping and localization<w:id w:val="116271404"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1].
Commercial Multi-Robot Systems
Commercial systems such as those developed by Amazon Robotics illustrate the feasibility of deploying large-scale multi-robot systems in real-world environments. These systems rely on centralized coordination combined with distributed execution, enabling efficient and reliable operation. This approach aligns with our current master-robot implementation and supports the planned expansion to multiple robots in future project phases<w:id w:val="1125961193"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2].
Open-Source Mapping Robot Projects
A review of open-source robotic projects available on platforms such as GitHub provided valuable practical insights. These projects revealed common hardware choices, typical sensor integration patterns, and frequent challenges encountered during SLAM-based navigation. Many of the documented solutions influenced our implementation decisions, particularly in areas such as power management, sensor calibration, and software modularity.
2.3 Review of Search and Rescue Robotic Systems
Search and Rescue (SAR) robotics is an important application domain for autonomous and semi-autonomous robotic systems, particularly in environments that are dangerous, inaccessible, or time-critical for human responders. The literature review of SAR robotic systems was conducted to understand the operational requirements, system architectures, and technological solutions commonly adopted in this field. The findings from this review directly informed several design decisions in our project, especially in relation to sensing, navigation, and system robustness<w:id w:val="280618667"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1].
2.3.1 Existing SAR Robot Systems
Urban Search and Rescue (USAR) Robots
Urban Search and Rescue (USAR) robots are designed to operate in complex and hazardous environments such as collapsed buildings, fire-affected areas, and industrial accident sites. Both commercial platforms and research prototypes emphasize robustness and operator safety as primary design goals.
From the reviewed USAR systems, several recurring requirements were identified:
Rugged mechanical design: Robots must withstand dust, debris, uneven terrain, and physical impacts.
Multi-sensor perception: The use of complementary sensors such as cameras, LiDAR, thermal sensors, and gas detectors enables better situational awareness.
Remote operation and supervision: Many USAR robots are teleoperated or supervised remotely to keep human operators out of dangerous zones.
Environmental hazard detection: Sensors for detecting smoke, toxic gases, or structural risks are critical for ensuring responder safety.
These characteristics influenced our project’s focus on environmental sensing (gas detection), remote monitoring through a dashboard, and the use of LiDAR and vision sensors to enhance environmental awareness<w:id w:val="1557970971"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1],<w:id w:val="-760449827"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4].
2.3.2 Key Features Identified
Based on the review of existing Search and Rescue (SAR) robotic systems, several core features were identified as essential to the proposed project. These features directly support effective operation in time-critical and hazardous environments.
One of the most important capabilities is rapid mapping, which enables the system to quickly generate an environmental representation of the operating area. This allows operators to gain situational awareness early in the mission and supports navigation and decision-making in unknown or dynamic environments.
Another critical feature is human detection, as locating survivors is a primary objective in SAR operations. The system must be capable of identifying human presence within the environment to assist rescue teams in prioritizing response efforts and planning safe extraction.
In addition, hazard awareness plays a vital role in ensuring mission safety. The ability to detect dangerous conditions or obstacles helps protect the robotic platform from damage and reduces risk to human responders who may later enter the environment.
Finally, real-time monitoring and reliable communication are essential for effective system operation. Operators require continuous feedback on robot status and sensor data, which depends on stable and dependable communication links, particularly in challenging and unpredictable SAR environments.
2.4 Review of SLAM Techniques
2.4.1 SLAM Fundamentals
Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM) is a fundamental technology for autonomous mobile robots operating in unknown environments. It enables a robot to construct a map of its surroundings while simultaneously estimating its own position within that map. This capability is especially important in search and rescue scenarios, where prior knowledge of the environment is often unavailable or unreliable.
The SLAM problem is inherently challenging due to the strong interdependence between mapping and localization. An accurate map cannot be created without precise knowledge of the robot’s position, while reliable localization depends on the availability of a consistent map. As a result, both processes must be solved concurrently. To address this challenge, SLAM systems typically rely on probabilistic estimation methods that combine sensor measurements and motion data to incrementally refine both the map and the robot’s pose over time<w:id w:val="-1245172664"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[15],<w:id w:val="-367374082"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5].
2.4.2 SLAM Algorithm Review
Several SLAM algorithms were reviewed to determine the most suitable approach for the proposed system, with particular emphasis on compatibility with the Robot Operating System (ROS), computational requirements, and sensor suitability<w:id w:val="247311958"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[8].
GMapping, which is based on the FastSLAM approach using particle filters, was identified as a strong candidate. It is widely used within the ROS ecosystem and supports two-dimensional occupancy grid mapping using LiDAR sensors. GMapping benefits from extensive documentation, tutorials, and community support, which makes it reliable and practical for academic and real-world applications. Its proven performance in numerous robotic projects and its suitability for real-time 2D mapping made it an appropriate choice for this project. Based on these considerations, GMapping was selected for implementation.
Hector SLAM was also reviewed as an alternative approach. Unlike GMapping, Hector SLAM does not rely heavily on wheel odometry and instead focuses on high-frequency scan matching. While this can be advantageous in certain scenarios, it typically requires high-quality and high-rate LiDAR data. Given the scope of the project and the availability of odometry information, GMapping was preferred due to its flexibility and stronger documentation support.
Additionally, Google’s Cartographer was reviewed to gain insight into more modern SLAM systems. Developed by Google, Cartographer supports both 2D and 3D mapping and offers high accuracy through advanced optimization techniques. However, it has significantly higher computational requirements and increased system complexity. As a result, it was not selected for implementation but was included in the review to understand current trends and advancements in SLAM research<w:id w:val="-2142113322"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5],<w:id w:val="-2087526315"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[9].
Different SLAM techniques have been proposed depending on sensor availability and operating environments. These techniques are summarized in Table 2.3.

2.4.3 Sensor Technologies for SLAM
A variety of sensor technologies are commonly used to support SLAM in mobile robotic systems. The selection of appropriate sensors has a direct impact on mapping accuracy, localization reliability, and overall system performance<w:id w:val="-1700695939"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[15].
Two-dimensional LiDAR (2D LiDAR) sensors are widely used in indoor and structured environments. These sensors provide distance measurements in a single horizontal plane, which makes them well suited for detecting walls, obstacles, and free space. Due to their compatibility with occupancy grid mapping techniques, 2D LiDAR sensors are frequently used in ROS-based SLAM systems. In addition, most commercial 2D LiDAR sensors are well supported within the ROS ecosystem, allowing for straightforward integration and real-time data processing. Although range and accuracy vary between different models, 2D LiDAR remains a practical and effective choice for generating reliable maps in indoor search and rescue scenarios.
An Inertial Measurement Unit (IMU) provides information related to orientation, angular velocity, and linear acceleration. IMU data plays an important role in improving localization accuracy, particularly during rotational motion or when wheel slippage occurs. By providing short-term motion estimates, the IMU helps compensate for errors that may arise from wheel encoders alone. When fused with other sensor data, IMU measurements contribute to more stable and accurate pose estimation.
Wheel encoders are another essential sensor for mobile robots, as they measure wheel rotation and enable the estimation of distance traveled and relative motion, commonly referred to as odometry. While wheel encoders provide continuous motion feedback, their measurements are prone to cumulative error over time due to factors such as wheel slip, uneven terrain, and mechanical imperfections. For this reason, encoder data is most effective when combined with additional sensors rather than being used independently.
2.4.4 Sensor Fusion
To overcome the limitations of individual sensors, sensor fusion techniques are commonly employed in SLAM systems. The literature review focused on probabilistic fusion methods, with particular attention given to the Extended Kalman Filter (EKF). EKF-based approaches allow data from multiple sensors—such as wheel encoders and IMUs—to be combined into a single, consistent estimate of the robot’s state<w:id w:val="-558010296"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[12].
Within the ROS framework, sensor fusion is commonly implemented using the robot_localization package, which provides EKF-based state estimation for mobile robots. Proper sensor calibration and accurate configuration are critical to achieving reliable fusion results. When correctly implemented, sensor fusion significantly improves localization accuracy and robustness compared to relying on any single sensor, making it well suited for the uncertain and dynamic environments encountered in search and rescue operations<w:id w:val="-542062338"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[13].
2.5 Review of Object Detection in Robotics
2.5.1 Deep Learning for Object Detection
Object detection has become a critical capability in modern robotic systems, particularly for tasks that require interaction with or identification of objects and humans in the environment. Recent advancements in this field are largely driven by deep learning techniques, especially Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), which have demonstrated strong performance in visual perception tasks.
Early object detection methods relied on handcrafted features such as edge detection, color histograms, and texture descriptors. While these approaches were computationally efficient, they were limited in their ability to generalize across varying environments and lighting conditions. A major shift occurred with the introduction of deep learning–based methods, which enabled automatic feature extraction directly from raw image data. Since then, object detection frameworks have evolved to directly predict object classes and bounding boxes in a single pipeline, resulting in significantly improved accuracy and robustness. Modern object detection systems now achieve high performance on standard benchmark datasets and are widely adopted in real-time robotic applications<w:id w:val="1386987823"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[11].
2.5.2 YOLO (You Only Look Once)
Among the reviewed object detection frameworks, YOLO (You Only Look Once) was studied in detail due to its suitability for real-time robotic systems. YOLO is a single-shot detection algorithm that processes an entire image in one forward pass of the neural network, allowing it to achieve high inference speeds compared to multi-stage detectors. This real-time performance makes it particularly well suited for time-sensitive applications such as search and rescue operations.
YOLO also benefits from the availability of pre-trained models capable of detecting common object classes, including humans. This reduces the need for extensive dataset collection and training, which aligns well with the scope and constraints of the project. In addition, YOLO has undergone continuous development, with successive versions introducing improvements in accuracy, efficiency, and ease of deployment.
Based on the literature review, YOLO was selected for implementation in this project due to its ability to reliably detect humans, its compatibility with Python-based systems, and its capability to run on the available hardware. These characteristics make it an effective and practical solution for supporting human detection within a robotic search and rescue framework<w:id w:val="-521091222"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[16],<w:id w:val="368808736"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[10].
Deep learning-based object detection models are widely used in robotic perception systems. A comparison of commonly used models is presented in Table 2.4.
Table 2.4: Comparison of object detection models used in robotic perception
