2.5.3 Deployment Considerations
Deploying deep learning–based object detection on mobile robots introduces several practical considerations that directly affect system performance and reliability. One of the primary concerns is computational requirements, as modern object detection models demand significant processing power. This constraint influences model selection and deployment strategy, particularly when operating on resource-limited robotic platforms.
Another important consideration is edge computing, where inference can be performed either onboard the robot or offloaded to a more powerful external computer. Onboard processing reduces dependency on communication links but is limited by available hardware, while offboard processing can offer higher performance at the cost of increased communication dependency. Closely related to this is latency, as detection speed must be sufficient to support real-time decision-making and safe robot operation. Excessive delay can reduce the usefulness of detection results in dynamic environments.
Finally, effective integration of object detection outputs with other system components is essential. Detection results must be properly synchronized with robot control, mapping, and monitoring systems to ensure that perception data can be used meaningfully within the overall robotic framework.
2.6 Review of Communication Frameworks
2.6.1 Robot Operating System (ROS)
The Robot Operating System (ROS) was a central focus of the literature review, as it forms the backbone of the proposed system’s software architecture. ROS provides a flexible and modular framework for developing robotic applications, enabling seamless integration of sensing, control, perception, and communication components.
ROS is built around a distributed communication model in which nodes exchange data using a publish–subscribe mechanism. This allows sensor data, control commands, and system states to be shared efficiently across different components. In addition, ROS supports service-based communication for synchronous operations and offers an extensive ecosystem of reusable packages. A wide range of development and debugging tools, including visualization and simulation environments, further enhance its suitability for robotic research and development. The strong community support and comprehensive documentation also make ROS particularly well suited for academic projects.
As part of the review, several ROS packages relevant to the project were studied. These include gmapping for SLAM implementation, robot_localization for sensor fusion using the Extended Kalman Filter, the navigation stack for path planning and motion control, tf for managing coordinate transformations, and rviz for three-dimensional visualization. Together, these packages provide a solid foundation for building an integrated robotic system<w:id w:val="-624625784"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
2.6.2 Network Communication
Network communication plays a critical role in enabling data exchange between robots and operator interfaces. The literature review examined common wireless communication solutions used in robotic systems, with a focus on WiFi-based communication.
WiFi is widely adopted due to its availability and ease of integration with ROS. The 2.4 GHz band offers longer range but is more susceptible to interference, while the 5 GHz band provides higher bandwidth and reduced interference at the cost of shorter range. Based on these trade-offs and the expected operating environment, 5 GHz WiFi was selected for the project implementation.
The review also considered transport-layer protocols, particularly TCP and UDP. TCP provides reliable data delivery with guaranteed packet ordering, which is advantageous for transmitting critical information such as maps and status updates. Although UDP offers lower latency, its lack of delivery guarantees makes it less suitable for reliable system communication. For this reason, TCP was selected to ensure dependable data transmission between the robots and the monitoring dashboard<w:id w:val="-1077514419"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4].
2.6.3 Dashboard and Monitoring
Effective dashboard and monitoring systems are essential for supervising robotic operations, particularly in search and rescue scenarios where real-time situational awareness is critical. The literature emphasizes that operators must be able to observe robot status, sensor data, and perception outputs in a clear and responsive manner to support informed decision-making during missions.
Web-based dashboards are widely adopted in robotic systems due to their cross-platform accessibility and ease of use. By leveraging modern web technologies, monitoring interfaces can be accessed through standard devices without the need for specialized software. This approach improves system usability and enables remote supervision of robotic platforms in diverse operating environments.
Integration between robotic systems and web-based dashboards is commonly achieved through ROS-compatible communication frameworks. ROS web bridge technologies enable real-time data exchange between ROS nodes and external interfaces, allowing monitoring applications to receive live updates from the robot. Communication protocols such as WebSockets are frequently used to support continuous, low-latency data transmission, which is necessary for displaying time-sensitive information such as robot status, sensor readings, and perception results.
Map and environment visualization are also key components of effective monitoring systems. The literature highlights the importance of presenting SLAM outputs and spatial information in an intuitive format that allows operators to understand both the robot’s surroundings and its position within the environment. Proper visualization of occupancy grid maps, combined with perception and system state information, enhances situational awareness and supports safer and more efficient operation.
Overall, dashboard and monitoring frameworks play a critical role in bridging the gap between autonomous robotic systems and human operators. By enabling real-time observation, interaction, and visualization, these systems support reliable supervision and contribute significantly to the effectiveness of robotic platforms in complex and dynamic environments<w:id w:val="15509957"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
2.7 Review of Hardware Components
Before selecting and purchasing hardware components, we conducted a comprehensive review of commonly used platforms and devices in mobile robotic systems, as illustrated in Figure 2.1. The aim of this review was to identify hardware solutions that are reliable, well-supported, and suitable for the computational and operational requirements of the proposed system.

 Figure 2.1 – Main hardware components
2.7.1 Processing Platforms
Processing platforms play a central role in managing perception, communication, and control tasks in robotic systems. Single-board computers and microcontrollers are often used together to balance computational capability and real-time performance.
The Raspberry Pi was reviewed as a primary onboard computing platform due to its widespread use in robotics. The Raspberry Pi 4, particularly the 8 GB variant, offers sufficient processing power and memory to support ROS-based applications, including SLAM and high-level system coordination. The Raspberry Pi 3B+, while less powerful, was also considered suitable for secondary or supporting roles. Strong community support, extensive documentation, and the availability of general-purpose input/output (GPIO) pins make the Raspberry Pi a practical choice for integrating sensors and peripheral devices.
In addition to single-board computers, Arduino microcontrollers are commonly employed for low-level, real-time control tasks. The Arduino Mega was reviewed due to its large number of input/output pins, which allows it to interface with multiple sensors and motors simultaneously. Arduino platforms are particularly well suited for handling timing-critical operations such as motor control, and they benefit from a wide range of well-documented libraries that simplify development and integration.
2.7.2 Sensors
A variety of sensors are required to support perception, navigation, and obstacle avoidance in mobile robotic systems. The literature review focused on sensors that are widely adopted and compatible with ROS-based architectures.
Two-dimensional LiDAR sensors are commonly used for SLAM and environment mapping. The review considered factors such as sensing range, measurement accuracy, scan frequency, and ROS compatibility. These sensors provide reliable spatial information that is essential for building occupancy grid maps and supporting navigation tasks.
Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs) were reviewed for their role in providing orientation and motion data. Considerations included the number of sensing axes (such as 6-axis or 9-axis configurations), measurement accuracy, and ease of integration with other sensors. IMU data is particularly useful for improving localization performance when combined with odometry information.
Ultrasonic sensors were also reviewed as a low-cost solution for short-range obstacle detection. While they do not provide the same level of detail as LiDAR, ultrasonic sensors are effective for detecting nearby objects and can complement other sensors in close-range scenarios.
For visual perception, USB cameras were reviewed for use in object and human detection tasks. Key evaluation criteria included image resolution, frame rate, and compatibility with common image processing and deep learning libraries. Cameras provide essential visual information that supports high-level perception and interaction with the environment.
2.7.3 Motors and Drive Systems
The drive system is a critical component that directly affects robot stability and control. The literature review examined various motor and drivetrain configurations commonly used in mobile robots.
DC motors equipped with encoders were identified as a practical choice due to their ability to provide both actuation and motion feedback. Encoder feedback enables accurate odometry estimation, which is essential for localization and navigation. Motor drivers were also reviewed as necessary interface components that allow microcontrollers to control motor speed and direction safely and efficiently.
Different wheel configurations were evaluated, including two-wheel differential drive systems with caster wheels and four-wheel drive systems. Based on the review and initial evaluation, a four-wheel configuration was identified as advantageous due to its improved torque distribution, stability, and suitability for carrying additional payloads.
2.7.4 Power Systems
The power system is responsible for supplying stable and sufficient energy to all hardware components. The literature review considered common power solutions used in mobile robotics.
Lithium polymer (LiPo) batteries were identified as a widely used option due to their high energy density and ability to deliver the current required by motors and onboard electronics. Voltage regulation was also highlighted as an important consideration, as different components often operate at different voltage levels. Proper regulation ensures reliable operation and protects sensitive electronics.
Safety considerations were emphasized throughout the review. The inclusion of fuses, protection circuits, and proper wiring practices was identified as essential for preventing damage to components and ensuring safe system operation.
2.8 Review of Mechanical Design Approaches
Mechanical design plays a critical role in ensuring the robustness, stability, and practicality of mobile robotic systems. The literature review focused on common chassis design strategies, material selection, manufacturing methods, and structured design workflows used in mobile robotics<w:id w:val="1389233566"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[17].
2.8.1 Chassis Design
The chassis serves as the primary structural component of the robot, supporting all hardware elements and protecting them during operation. Various material options are commonly used in robotic chassis design, including aluminum, plastics, wood, and additively manufactured components. Each material presents trade-offs in terms of strength, weight, manufacturability, and cost.
Aluminum sheets are frequently adopted in mobile robot designs due to their favorable strength-to-weight ratio, durability, and resistance to deformation. In addition, aluminum is compatible with several fabrication techniques such as cutting, bending, and drilling, making it suitable for both prototyping and final designs. Based on these considerations, aluminum sheet was identified as an appropriate choice for achieving a durable and stable chassis within the constraints of the project.
The review also examined commonly used manufacturing methods, including CNC cutting, laser cutting, 3D printing, and manual fabrication. For planar metal structures, cutting and bending aluminum sheets was found to be an efficient and accessible approach that balances precision and practicality.
In terms of design tools, computer-aided design (CAD) software is essential for visualizing, validating, and refining mechanical structures prior to fabrication. Software packages such as AutoCAD, SolidWorks, Fusion 360, and Inventor are widely used in robotic design. Inventor was selected for 3D modeling due to its availability and the abundance of learning resources, enabling effective design development and documentation.
2.8.2 Design Workflow
The literature review emphasized the importance of adopting a structured mechanical design workflow to reduce fabrication errors and support iterative improvement. A typical workflow begins with creating a detailed three-dimensional chassis model using CAD software, allowing designers to verify dimensions, component placement, and structural integrity.
Once the 3D design is finalized, it is converted into two-dimensional technical drawings suitable for fabrication. These drawings guide the cutting and bending processes used to shape the aluminum sheets. Following fabrication, the chassis components are assembled using mechanical fasteners, ensuring modularity and ease of maintenance.
Finally, iterative refinement is an essential aspect of mechanical design. Testing the assembled chassis under operational conditions allows design limitations to be identified and addressed through incremental modifications. This iterative approach supports continuous improvement and helps ensure that the final mechanical structure meets functional and durability requirements.
2.9 Summary of Literature Review
This literature review provided a comprehensive understanding of the technologies, design approaches, and system architectures relevant to the development of a search and rescue robotic system. Through the analysis of existing research and practical implementations, we were able to identify suitable solutions that align with the objectives and constraints of our graduation project.
From a software and communication perspective, the review highlighted the Robot Operating System (ROS) as a robust and widely adopted framework for robot control, perception, and inter-module communication. For mapping and localization, GMapping was identified as an appropriate SLAM solution due to its proven reliability, compatibility with ROS, and suitability for two-dimensional LiDAR-based environments. In the area of perception, YOLO-based object detection methods emerged as a practical choice for real-time human detection, offering a balance between accuracy and computational efficiency.
Hardware-related studies guided our selection of processing and control platforms. The combination of a single-board computer and a microcontroller was found to be effective in separating high-level processing tasks from real-time control operations. Communication technologies reviewed in the literature emphasized the importance of reliable wireless connectivity, leading to the selection of 5 GHz WiFi and TCP-based communication to ensure stable and dependable data transmission between system components<w:id w:val="-1983228446"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1].
The literature review also informed several key design decisions. A multi-robot architecture was identified as advantageous for distributing tasks and improving system flexibility. Mechanical design studies supported the use of a four-wheel drive configuration to enhance stability and torque, while aluminum chassis designs were shown to provide durability and structural integrity. Additionally, the importance of centralized dashboards for monitoring and supervision was emphasized as a means of improving operator situational awareness.
In addition to guiding technology and design choices, the literature review allowed us to anticipate several challenges likely to be encountered during development. These include the complexity of electronic circuit design, the iterative nature of mechanical development, the difficulty of integrating multiple heterogeneous sensors, and the dependence of system performance on reliable network infrastructure.
Overall, this literature review established a strong theoretical foundation for the project by identifying proven technologies, design best practices, and potential challenges. The insights gained directly informed the system requirements and architecture, providing a clear transition to the system analysis and design phase presented in the following chapter<w:id w:val="-1011982385"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1].

Chapter 3

Chapter 3: System Analysis and Design
3.1 Project Planning and Feasibility
The planning of this graduation project was carried out with a strong focus on practicality, scalability, and alignment with academic objectives. The project aims to develop an autonomous robotic system capable of navigating unknown environments, generating maps, detecting objects, and sensing hazardous gases. Due to the complexity of robotic systems and the limitations of time and resources within an academic setting, careful planning was essential to ensure successful implementation.
Project feasibility was evaluated from multiple perspectives. Technical feasibility was assessed by analyzing the availability of mature robotic frameworks such as ROS 2, proven LiDAR-based mapping techniques, and lightweight deep learning models suitable for real-time object detection. Time feasibility was ensured by dividing the project into manageable phases distributed over an eight-month period. Resource feasibility was considered by selecting hardware components and software tools that are accessible, cost-effective, and well-documented.
The planning process also accounted for future expansion. While the current implementation focuses on a single master robot, the system is designed with scalability in mind to allow the integration of additional robots in later phases. This long-term vision influenced early design decisions, particularly in system modularity and communication architecture<w:id w:val="1889993729"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1]<w:id w:val="-1521391226"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
3.1.1 Development Methodology
The development of the proposed autonomous robotic system follows an Agile, incremental, and iterative development methodology, which is particularly well-suited for complex cyber-physical systems such as autonomous robots. Robotic systems inherently combine hardware, software, sensing, and control, all of which are subject to continuous refinement as system behavior is observed in real-world conditions. For this reason, a traditional linear development model would impose significant risk, as early design decisions may require revision after integration and testing.
Agile Approach Rationale
An Agile-oriented approach was selected to allow continuous adaptation and progressive system evolution. Rather than attempting to design and implement the entire system upfront, the project is developed through short, structured iterations that enable frequent validation and refinement. This approach supports rapid feedback cycles, early identification of integration issues, and flexible response to hardware limitations or unexpected system behavior.
The Agile methodology is particularly advantageous for robotics projects, where interactions between mechanical design, sensor accuracy, computational constraints, and environmental conditions cannot be fully predicted at the design stage. By adopting an iterative process, the development team can continuously assess system performance and adjust design decisions accordingly<w:id w:val="35482680"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
Sprint Structure
The project is divided into multiple development iterations, referred to as sprints, each with clearly defined objectives and deliverables. Each sprint focuses on a specific functional layer of the system, starting from foundational components and progressing toward higher-level autonomous capabilities.
Early sprints were dedicated to mechanical construction, motor integration, and basic robot mobility. Subsequent sprints introduced sensor integration, including LiDAR, camera, and gas sensor setup, followed by the implementation of mapping, localization, and visualization functionalities. Later sprints focused on perception features such as object detection using YOLOv8n, dashboard development, and system monitoring.
Each sprint follows a structured lifecycle that includes planning, implementation, testing, and evaluation. At the end of each sprint, implemented features are validated through practical experiments, and identified issues are documented for resolution in subsequent iterations. This structured sprint-based approach ensures continuous progress while maintaining system stability.
Systems Engineering Integration
The development methodology incorporates principles of systems engineering, ensuring that the robot is treated as an integrated system rather than a collection of independent components. System-level requirements guide the design of individual subsystems, including hardware, software, sensing, and communication.
Throughout development, careful attention is given to subsystem interfaces and data flow. Changes introduced in one component are evaluated for their impact on the overall system, minimizing integration conflicts. This systems engineering perspective ensures coherence between mechanical design, software architecture, and operational objectives.
Additionally, incremental integration is emphasized, where new subsystems are introduced gradually and tested in isolation before being fully integrated into the complete system. This approach reduces integration complexity and improves overall system reliability<w:id w:val="61068935"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2] .
Team Organization
The development team is organized into specialized subteams to enable parallel progress across the different technical domains of the project. This structured organization ensures efficient task distribution while maintaining coherence across the overall system design.
The mechanical and hardware subteam, consisting of two members, is responsible for the physical design and construction of the four-wheel robot platform. Their objectives include chassis design, motor installation, sensor mounting, power management, and ensuring mechanical stability and reliability of the robot.
The software and robotics subteam, consisting of two members, focuses on implementing the core system functionality using ROS 2. Their responsibilities include robot control, sensor data integration, mapping and localization, and system-level testing. This subteam ensures that the robot operates autonomously and that all software components interact correctly.
The perception and dashboard subteam, consisting of one member, is responsible for visual perception and user interaction. This includes integrating the Logitech camera, implementing object detection using YOLOv8n on the external laptop, and developing the NiceGUI-based dashboard for real-time monitoring and visualization.
Although responsibilities are divided among subteams, regular coordination meetings are conducted to ensure alignment between hardware and software development. This collaborative structure facilitates knowledge sharing, enables early identification of cross-domain dependencies, and ensures that system integration proceeds smoothly. As a result, hardware and software development progress in parallel, reducing delays associated with sequential development and enhancing overall project efficiency.
Coordination Mechanisms
Effective coordination mechanisms are essential for managing the complexity of the project and ensuring smooth collaboration among team members. Regular progress reviews are conducted at the end of each development sprint to evaluate completed tasks, verify system functionality, and identify technical challenges encountered during implementation. These reviews help assess milestone achievement and guide planning for subsequent sprints.
Project documentation is continuously maintained to record system architecture decisions, hardware configurations, software updates, and testing results. This documentation supports knowledge sharing among team members and ensures continuity throughout the development process.
GitHub is used as the primary version control and collaboration platform for managing the project’s software components. All source code, configuration files, and documentation are stored in a centralized GitHub repository, enabling controlled code updates, change tracking, and rollback when necessary. This approach ensures consistency across contributions and facilitates collaborative development.
Clear and direct communication channels are established among team members to support rapid issue resolution and effective decision-making. These coordination mechanisms collectively ensure efficient collaboration, maintain alignment with project objectives, and support adherence to the planned development timeline.
3.1.2 Project Timeline and Gantt Chart
The project is planned over a total duration of 8 months, structured to ensure steady progress and balanced workload distribution. At the time of writing this report, the project has completed approximately four months of development.
The timeline is organized as follows:
Month 1: Design and construction of the four-wheel mobile robot, including chassis assembly and motor integration.
Month 2: Installation and configuration of the ROS 2 environment, along with implementation of basic robot motion and sensor communication.
Month 3: Integration of the LiDAR sensor and implementation of mapping and visualization using RViz2.
Month 4: Development of the NiceGUI dashboard, integration of YOLOv8n-based object detection, gas sensing using the MQ-2 sensor, and initial setup of a virtual simulation environment.
Months 5–8 (Planned): Development of a second robot, system coordination, advanced testing, performance optimization, and documentation completion.
A Gantt chart is used to visually represent task scheduling, dependencies, and progress. This chart helps track milestones, manage overlapping tasks, and ensure adherence to the planned timeline.
3.2 Problem Analysis and Need for the System
From a business and organizational perspective, many sectors such as emergency response agencies, industrial operators, and infrastructure management organizations face significant challenges when operating in hazardous or unknown environments. These organizations require reliable, fast, and accurate situational awareness to support decision-making while minimizing operational risk and cost. However, current inspection and monitoring practices rely heavily on human presence, which exposes personnel to danger, increases operational costs, and limits the frequency and scope of inspections.
Organizations operating in disaster response and industrial safety require systems that can collect environmental data autonomously, navigate complex spaces without prior maps, and provide real-time feedback to decision-makers. Manual inspection methods are often inefficient, requiring extensive preparation, protective equipment, and trained personnel. Additionally, human-based inspections are subject to fatigue, limited visibility, and inconsistent data collection, which can negatively impact operational effectiveness and reliability.
The primary need from a business perspective is the availability of a cost-effective, autonomous, and scalable system that can operate continuously in hazardous environments while delivering accurate and timely information. Such a system must reduce reliance on human intervention, improve operational efficiency, and support faster response times during critical situations. Furthermore, organizations require systems that are adaptable and extensible, allowing future upgrades without complete redesign.
However, several limitations exist in current solutions. Many available robotic systems are expensive, require complex infrastructure, or are tailored for highly specific use cases, making them unsuitable for small- to medium-scale organizations. Additionally, systems that lack real-time mapping, autonomous navigation, or environmental sensing capabilities are insufficient for dynamic and unstructured environments.
To address these limitations, the proposed system introduces an autonomous mobile robot capable of simultaneous localization and mapping, autonomous navigation, object detection, and hazardous gas sensing. By integrating these functionalities into a single platform, the system provides comprehensive environmental awareness while maintaining operational simplicity. The use of modular hardware and open-source software further enhances affordability and flexibility.
The system is designed to meet specific operational needs, including real-time environment mapping, autonomous movement without predefined paths, visual object recognition, and gas detection for safety monitoring. Additionally, the system supports remote monitoring through a dashboard interface, enabling decision-makers to assess conditions without direct exposure. The planned expansion to a multi-robot configuration further addresses scalability requirements by enabling wider area coverage and improved system robustness<w:id w:val="1393469320"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1]<w:id w:val="30390955"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4].
3.3 Requirements and Users
The requirements of the autonomous robotic system were defined based on real operational needs observed in environments where human access is limited, risky, or inefficient. Throughout the development process, the system was treated as a practical solution intended to support organizations that require continuous monitoring, inspection, and environmental awareness, rather than as a purely academic prototype.
The implemented requirements reflect the need for autonomous navigation, real-time mapping, environmental sensing, and remote monitoring. These capabilities were selected to address real-life challenges faced by facilities such as hospitals, industrial firms, warehouses, and other large indoor environments, where safety, efficiency, and reliability are critical. The system design prioritizes features that reduce human exposure to risk, minimize manual intervention, and provide accurate information to decision-makers<w:id w:val="-1541274852"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
3.3.1 User and Stakeholder Description
The primary users of the system are operators and supervisors working in facilities such as hospitals, industrial firms, and other large operational environments. These users rely on the robot to navigate autonomously, collect environmental data, and provide real-time feedback through a monitoring dashboard. In a hospital environment, the system can assist in monitoring restricted areas, detecting hazards, or inspecting corridors without disrupting daily operations. In industrial or commercial facilities, the robot can support routine inspections, safety checks, and environmental monitoring tasks.
Another group of users includes technical staff and system operators, who are responsible for deploying the robot, initiating missions, and monitoring system performance. These users interact with the system primarily through the dashboard, which displays the generated map, robot position, camera feed, object detection results, and connectivity status. The system is designed to minimize the need for direct human control, allowing operators to focus on supervision rather than manual navigation.
Stakeholders also include organizations and facility management teams that benefit from improved safety and operational efficiency. These stakeholders are interested in reducing inspection costs, minimizing downtime, and enhancing safety compliance through automated monitoring solutions. The system addresses these needs by providing a scalable and adaptable platform that can be integrated into existing workflows.
Academic supervisors and the Faculty of Engineering at May University in Cairo remain important stakeholders in the current phase, as they oversee development quality and evaluation. However, the project is explicitly designed to extend beyond academic use, offering a foundation for real-world deployment and future enhancements.
3.3.2 Functional Requirements
The functional requirements of the system describe the specific operations and capabilities that the autonomous robotic system is required to perform based on its current implementation. These requirements are derived directly from the functionalities that have been developed and tested using the master robot and its associated monitoring dashboard <w:id w:val="-831443967"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="A33ADC05F83741DCA18E7E84419ADFF7"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5]<w:id w:val="-440378740"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="A33ADC05F83741DCA18E7E84419ADFF7"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[11].
Autonomous Navigation The system shall enable the robot to move autonomously within indoor environments without requiring continuous manual control. The robot must be able to traverse corridors and open spaces while responding to environmental constraints detected through onboard sensors.
Environment Mapping The system shall generate a two-dimensional map of the surrounding environment using LiDAR data. This map must be continuously updated as the robot explores new areas, allowing operators to visualize the environment in real time.
Localization The system shall estimate the robot’s position and orientation within the generated map during operation. Accurate localization is required to track robot movement and support safe navigation in structured environments such as hospitals or industrial facilities.
Obstacle Detection and Avoidance The robot shall detect obstacles present in its path using sensor data and adjust its motion to avoid collisions. This functionality is essential for safe operation in environments where people, equipment, or furniture may be present.
Visual Data Acquisition The system shall capture live video streams from the onboard Logitech camera and transmit them to an external computing device for further processing and visualization.
Object Detection and Classification The system shall perform real-time object detection using the YOLOv8n model executed on an external laptop. Detected objects must be identified and displayed through the monitoring dashboard to enhance situational awareness.
Gas Detection The system shall monitor environmental conditions using the MQ-2 gas sensor and provide real-time gas concentration readings. This functionality supports early detection of hazardous conditions in enclosed or sensitive environments.
System Monitoring and Visualization The system shall provide a graphical dashboard that displays key operational data, including the generated map, robot position, camera feed with classification results, and connectivity status. This enables remote supervision and informed decision-making.
Data Communication The system shall support continuous data exchange between the robot and the external monitoring device using ROS 2 communication mechanisms. Sensor data and system status information must be transmitted reliably during operation.
Support for Future Multi-Robot Expansion The system shall be designed to allow integration of additional robots in future development phases. The master robot must be capable of sharing perception and sensing information with secondary robots when this functionality is implemented.
These requirements ensure that the system fulfills its intended operational objectives.
3.3.3 Non-Functional Requirements
The non-functional requirements define the quality attributes and operational constraints of the autonomous robotic system. These requirements describe how the system should perform under real operating conditions and ensure that the system is reliable, usable, and suitable for deployment in safety-critical environments<w:id w:val="-2138251062"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4].
Reliability The system shall operate continuously during missions without unexpected shutdowns or failures. Sensor data acquisition, mapping, and communication must remain stable throughout operation to ensure consistent performance in real-world environments.
Real-Time Performance The system shall process sensor data and update visualizations with minimal delay. Map generation, robot position updates, and dashboard information must be refreshed in near real time to allow operators to respond promptly to changing conditions.
Safety The robot shall operate safely in environments where people, equipment, and sensitive infrastructure may be present. Motion control and obstacle avoidance must prevent collisions, and gas sensing data must be clearly presented to support timely safety decisions.
Usability The dashboard interface shall be intuitive and easy to understand for operators with minimal technical background. Information displayed must be clearly organized, readable, and accessible to support effective supervision.
Scalability The system architecture shall support future expansion to multi-robot operation without requiring major redesign. Software components must be modular to allow additional robots to be integrated in subsequent development phases.
Maintainability The system shall be designed to allow easy maintenance and updates. Software modules should be independently modifiable, and hardware components should be accessible for inspection or replacement.
Interoperability The system shall be compatible with standard robotic tools and frameworks. The use of ROS 2 ensures that the system can integrate with additional sensors, software packages, or external systems if required.
Robustness The system shall tolerate minor sensor noise, communication delays, and environmental variations without significant degradation in performance. This requirement is particularly important for operation in dynamic or cluttered environments.
Portability The system software shall be deployable on different computing platforms, including onboard controllers and external laptops. This ensures flexibility in adapting the system to different hardware configurations.
Meeting these requirements ensures long-term system effectiveness and usability.
3.4 System Architecture
The system architecture is designed to support reliable operation, modular development, and future scalability. The architecture reflects the current implementation of a single master robot while allowing planned expansion to a multi-robot configuration. It is structured to clearly separate sensing, processing, control, perception, and visualization components, ensuring that each subsystem operates efficiently and can be developed or modified independently.
At the core of the system architecture is the master robot, which performs real-time sensing, motion execution, and environment interaction. The robot is equipped with onboard sensors, including a LiDAR sensor, a camera, and a gas sensor, which continuously collect data from the environment. These sensors provide the raw inputs required for mapping, localization, perception, and safety monitoring.
The system is built around ROS 2 as the primary middleware, which manages communication between the different software components using a distributed, node-based architecture. Each major system functionality is implemented as an independent ROS 2 node or a group of cooperating nodes, allowing clear separation of responsibilities and modular system development. This design enables efficient and asynchronous exchange of sensor data, robot state information, and control commands while ensuring scalability and fault tolerance. The overall interaction between robots and software components within the swarm framework is illustrated in Figure 3.1.

Figure 3.1 – Multi-robot system configuration
Mapping and localization are handled by dedicated ROS 2 nodes that process LiDAR data to generate a real-time representation of the environment and estimate the robot’s position within it. The generated map and localization data are shared with other system components to support navigation and visualization. RViz2 is integrated into the architecture to provide real-time visualization of the map, robot pose, and sensor data during operation and testing, as illustrated in Figure 3.2.

Figure 3.2 – Multi-Robot System Architecture and Communication Framework
For perception tasks, the system architecture separates computation between the robot and an external laptop. The robot transmits the live camera stream to the laptop, where object detection is performed using the YOLOv8n model. This architectural decision reduces the computational load on the robot and allows the use of more powerful hardware for deep learning inference. The perception results are then displayed through the monitoring dashboard, enhancing situational awareness without affecting robot mobility performance.
The communication and monitoring layer is implemented through a custom dashboard developed using NiceGUI. This dashboard serves as the main interface between the system and the operator. It receives data from the robot via ROS 2 communication channels and presents it in a structured and user-friendly format. The dashboard displays the environment map, robot position, camera feed with classification results, gas sensor readings, and robot connectivity status.
From an architectural perspective, the system follows a distributed and modular design, where real-time control remains on the robot, while high-level perception and visualization are handled externally. This separation improves system reliability, simplifies debugging, and supports scalability. When a second robot is introduced in future phases, the architecture allows the master robot to share mapping, perception, and sensing data with the additional robot without fundamental changes to the system structure.
Overall, the system architecture ensures coherent integration of hardware and software components, supports real-time operation, and provides a solid foundation for future enhancements and real-world deployment in environments such as hospitals, industrial facilities, and other safety-critical locations<w:id w:val="-1576119074"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="90D5983C59A447FC9F535111F1C816A5"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
3.5 Hardware and Software Design
The hardware and software design of the autonomous robotic system were developed in parallel to ensure tight integration between physical components and computational processes. Design decisions were driven by real operational requirements, system reliability, and the need to support autonomous navigation, perception, and monitoring in real-world environments such as hospitals and industrial facilities. The system design emphasizes modularity, ease of integration, and extensibility for future development phases.
3.5.1 Hardware Design
The hardware design of the system is based on a modular mobile robotic platform that supports autonomous navigation, environment mapping, visual perception, and environmental monitoring. The overall design emphasizes robustness, scalability, and ease of maintenance, allowing the system to be extended into a multi-robot configuration in later development phases <w:id w:val="-991556911"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="5F660128C3A64BC291EAF78172841542"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[17] The mechanical structure and component layout of the platform are illustrated in Figure 3.3.

Figure 3.3 – CAD model of the robot chassis
Overall Design Approach
The robot platform is designed as a four-wheel differential-drive mobile base, a configuration selected to provide mechanical stability, controlled maneuverability, and reliable operation in indoor environments such as hospitals, offices, and industrial facilities. The front wheels are actively driven by individual DC motors, enabling differential steering and precise motion control, while the remaining wheels provide passive support and stability during movement. The final assembled hardware design of the robot platform is shown in Figure 3.4.

Figure 3.4 – Final Assembled Design of the Mapping Explorer Robot
The physical dimensions of the robot are approximately 40 cm × 30 cm × 25 cm, with an overall weight of around 10 kg per robot. These dimensions were chosen to allow the robot to navigate through narrow corridors and confined indoor spaces while carrying all required sensors and electronic components.
Mapping Explorer Robot
The Mapping Explorer Robot represents the master robot in the current system implementation. It is responsible for autonomous navigation, environment mapping, localization, and coordination with other system components.
Main Components

Visual Classifier Robot
The Visual Classifier Robot is designed to focus on visual perception tasks. It is intended to operate in coordination with the master robot in future development phases.
Processing Unit: Raspberry Pi 3B+
Sensor: USB HD camera capable of 1080p resolution at 30 frames per second
Localization: Does not include LiDAR; instead, localization is achieved using a combination of IMU data and wheel encoders, relying on a shared environment map provided by the master robot
This design reduces hardware complexity while maintaining sufficient perception capability for object classification tasks.
Environmental Sensing Robot
The Environmental Sensing Robot is designed to monitor environmental conditions, particularly air quality and hazardous gases.
Processing Unit: Raspberry Pi 3B+
Gas Sensors:
MQ-2: Detects smoke, butane, and methane gases
MQ-135: Measures air quality and detects harmful gases
Localization: Based on IMU and wheel encoder data
This robot focuses on environmental sensing and safety monitoring, complementing the capabilities of the master robot.
Sensor Specifications
The sensors used across the robotic system are selected to provide reliable performance while maintaining cost-effectiveness. Their specifications are summarized in Table 3.2:
Table 3.2: Specifications of sensors used in the robotic system.

3.5.2 Software Design
The software design of the autonomous robotic system is based on a layered and modular architecture implemented using ROS 2 as the core middleware. This design approach enables clear separation between hardware interaction, control logic, navigation, perception, and user interaction. The software architecture is structured to support real-time operation, system scalability, and ease of maintenance while accommodating the current single-robot implementation and planned multi-robot expansion<w:id w:val="-2096779978"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5] overall software stack and the interaction between ROS 2 nodes are illustrated in Figure 3.5.

Figure 3.5 – Software Stack and ROS Node Architecture
Overall Software Architecture
The software system is organized into five functional layers, each responsible for a specific aspect of system operation. This layered architecture improves system clarity and allows individual components to be developed, tested, and modified independently.
Layer 1: Hardware Drivers
This layer interfaces directly with the physical hardware components of the robot, including motors and sensors. It abstracts hardware-specific details and exposes standardized interfaces to higher-level software components. By isolating hardware dependencies, this layer ensures that changes in hardware do not significantly affect the rest of the software system.
Layer 2: Low-Level Control and Odometry
Low-level control and time-critical operations are handled using an Arduino-based control unit. This layer is responsible for real-time motor control, wheel encoder reading, IMU data acquisition, and basic odometry computation. Delegating these tasks to a microcontroller ensures deterministic timing and responsive motor behavior while reducing the computational load on the main processing platform.
Layer 3: Navigation and Localization
This layer handles spatial understanding and robot positioning. For the mapping robot, a 2D SLAM approach using GMapping is employed to process LiDAR data and generate an occupancy grid map of the environment. Localization is achieved through sensor fusion using an Extended Kalman Filter (EKF) that combines wheel odometry and IMU data to estimate the robot’s pose. Navigation-related functions such as waypoint following and obstacle avoidance operate within this layer to support autonomous movement. Robots that do not carry LiDAR rely on odometry and IMU data while referencing a shared environment map provided by the mapping robot.
Layer 4: High-Level Applications
High-level application logic is implemented in this layer. It includes object detection interfaces, hazard mapping functionality, and decision-making logic that governs robot behavior. This layer integrates perception results and sensor data to support intelligent system responses and mission-level behavior.
Layer 5: Communication and Dashboard
The top layer manages communication and user interaction. ROS publish–subscribe messaging is used to distribute data between system components, while a web-based dashboard provides real-time monitoring and visualization. This layer also supports data logging for analysis and debugging.
ROS Node Structure
Each robot in the system runs a specific set of ROS nodes based on its assigned role. This node-based structure allows functional separation and simplifies system integration.
Mapping Explorer Robot Nodes
The Mapping Explorer Robot is responsible for environment mapping, localization, and autonomous navigation. It runs the following ROS nodes:
Hardware Interface Node – Interfaces with motors, encoders, and onboard sensors.
LiDAR Node – Publishes laser scan data for mapping and obstacle detection.
IMU Node – Publishes inertial data for orientation estimation.
Odometry Node – Computes wheel-based odometry information.
SLAM Node – Executes the GMapping algorithm to generate and update the environment map.
Navigation Node – Handles path planning, goal execution, and motion commands.
Together, these nodes enable autonomous exploration, mapping, and navigation within unknown environments.
Visual Classifier Robot Nodes
The Visual Classifier Robot is dedicated to visual perception and object classification tasks. Its software configuration is optimized for camera-based sensing rather than full SLAM operation. The robot runs the following nodes:
Hardware Interface Node – Controls motors and basic sensor inputs.
Camera Node – Captures images from the USB camera and publishes image streams.
Odometry Node – Estimates motion using wheel encoder and IMU data.
Localization Support Node – Estimates robot position relative to a shared map generated by the mapping robot.
Perception Communication Node – Transmits visual data and detection-related information to the central processing unit or dashboard.
This structure allows the robot to perform efficient visual perception while relying on shared spatial information, reducing hardware complexity and computational requirements.
Environmental Sensing Robot Nodes
The Environmental Sensing Robot focuses on monitoring environmental conditions and detecting potential hazards. It runs the following nodes:
Hardware Interface Node – Manages motors and sensor connections.
Gas Sensor Node – Reads data from MQ-series gas sensors and publishes environmental measurements.
Odometry Node – Computes motion estimates from wheel encoders.
Localization Support Node – Estimates robot position using IMU and odometry data.
This node configuration enables continuous environmental monitoring and supports system-level safety awareness.
Laptop / Central Hub Nodes
Computationally intensive tasks and system-wide monitoring are executed on an external laptop acting as a central processing and visualization hub. The following nodes are implemented:
Map Server Node – Stores and distributes the generated environment map.
Object Detection Node – Executes deep learning–based object detection and publishes detection results.
Dashboard Server Node – Aggregates system data and provides a web-based monitoring interface.
Data Logging Node – Records sensor data, detection results, and mission events for evaluation.
This separation allows advanced processing and visualization to be performed without affecting the mobility and responsiveness of the robotic platforms and the technologies for each component is provided in the following table.
The software technologies and frameworks used in the proposed system are summarized in Table 3.3.
Table 3.3: Software technology stack used in the robotic system

3.6 Communication and Dashboard Design
Communication within the autonomous robotic system is designed to support reliable, real-time data exchange between the robots, the external computing device, and the monitoring dashboard. The communication architecture is implemented using ROS 2, which provides a publish–subscribe messaging model that enables distributed system components to exchange data efficiently and asynchronously<w:id w:val="-330838446"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4],<w:id w:val="1322395667"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6] The web-based interface used for system monitoring and data visualization is illustrated in Figure 3.6.

Figure 3.6 – Web-based monitoring dashboard
Communication Architecture
The communication architecture of the proposed robotic system is designed to support reliable, real-time data exchange between the mobile robot, external computing resources, and higher-level monitoring components. The architecture follows a distributed communication model, where different types of data are transmitted using mechanisms best suited to their timing, bandwidth, and reliability requirements.
At the core of the communication architecture is ROS-based message passing, which is used for structured and low-latency exchange of robot state, sensor measurements, and mapping data. ROS topics are employed to decouple data producers and consumers, allowing system components to operate independently while remaining synchronized. This publish–subscribe model ensures scalability and modularity, enabling additional nodes or robots to be integrated without redesigning the communication framework.
Intra-Robot Communication
Within the robot, communication between software components is handled locally through ROS nodes. Sensor drivers publish data such as LiDAR scans, IMU measurements, encoder readings, and odometry estimates to dedicated ROS topics. Control and navigation nodes subscribe to these topics to compute motion commands and update localization estimates. This internal communication remains fully onboard, ensuring fast response times for time-critical operations such as motion control and obstacle avoidance.
Robot-to-Laptop Communication
Communication between the robot and the external laptop is established over a wireless network. This connection is essential for transmitting data that either requires higher computational resources or is intended for visualization and monitoring.
Different communication strategies are used depending on data characteristics:
ROS topic communication is used for structured data such as maps, odometry, robot pose, gas sensor readings, and system status. These data streams are lightweight, well-defined, and benefit from ROS’s standardized message formats.
TCP-based video streaming is used for transmitting camera frames from the robot to the laptop. This approach is implemented to handle high-bandwidth visual data efficiently while minimizing latency. By avoiding raw image transmission through ROS, the system reduces communication overhead and ensures smoother real-time video delivery.
This hybrid communication approach balances reliability and performance, ensuring that critical control and state information remains stable while high-volume data is transmitted efficiently.
Centralized Processing and Data Distribution
The external laptop acts as a central processing and aggregation node within the communication architecture. It receives data streams from the robot, performs computationally intensive tasks such as object detection using YOLOv8, and redistributes processed information to other system components.
Detection results, system events, and monitoring data are then made available to higher-level applications, including the monitoring dashboard. This centralized processing model simplifies system management, reduces onboard computational load, and supports future expansion to multi-robot scenarios where multiple robots may communicate with the same central hub.
Message Rates and Data Flow
To ensure stable communication and efficient bandwidth usage, message publication rates are carefully selected based on sensor characteristics and system requirements:
LiDAR scans: 5.5 Hz
IMU data: Acquired at 100 Hz, published at approximately 10 Hz
Odometry: 20 Hz
Map updates: 1–3 Hz during active mapping
Camera stream:
10 FPS at full resolution
Up to 30 FPS using compressed transport
Gas sensor readings: 10 Hz
Velocity commands: Approximately 10 Hz, continuously published
These rates provide sufficient temporal resolution for navigation, perception, and monitoring while avoiding unnecessary network congestion.
Bandwidth Estimation
An approximate bandwidth analysis was conducted to verify that the communication load remains within practical limits for standard wireless networks:
LiDAR data: 5.5 Hz × 720 ranges × 4 bytes ≈ 16 KB/s
Camera stream (compressed): 10 FPS × 50 KB per frame ≈ 500 KB/s
Odometry and auxiliary data: Approximately 100 KB/s
The total estimated bandwidth usage is approximately 600 KB/s, which is well within the capacity of standard WiFi networks. This confirms that the system can operate reliably in indoor environments such as hospitals and industrial facilities.
Dashboard Design
The monitoring dashboard provides a real-time interface for supervising the autonomous robotic system, displaying essential information such as robot status, sensor data, navigation details, and alerts to support effective system monitoring and operator intervention when required, as shown in Figure 3.7.

Figure 3.7 – Real-Time Dashboard Interface
The dashboard is responsible for aggregating data from multiple ROS 2 topics and presenting it in a clear and organized manner. It enables real-time system supervision without requiring direct interaction with the robot or underlying ROS tools.
Dashboard Components
Map Visualization
Displays the occupancy grid map as a two-dimensional image
Shows robot positions as colored markers
Overlays hazard heat maps based on gas sensor readings
Robot Status Panel
Shows the connection status (online/offline)
Indicates Raspberry-pi ip address
Shows connection protocol and port
Detections Panel
Lists detected objects in real time
Displays object class, confidence score, and estimated location
Supports filtering by object type or confidence threshold
Dashboard Technology and Usability
The dashboard is developed using NiceGUI, enabling a web-based interface that can be accessed from the external laptop. This approach allows flexible deployment, platform independence, and easy extension of dashboard features. The interface is designed to prioritize clarity, responsiveness, and ease of use, ensuring that operators can quickly assess system status and respond to environmental changes.
Overall, the communication and dashboard design provide a reliable and efficient framework for real-time monitoring, control, and decision-making, supporting safe and effective operation of the robotic system in real-world environments.
3.7 Risks, Assumptions, and Dependencies
The development and deployment of the autonomous robotic system involve several risks, assumptions, and external dependencies that may influence system performance and project progress. These factors were identified during the design and implementation phases and were considered throughout the development process to reduce their impact on system reliability and project outcomes<w:id w:val="-2086600157"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1].
Risks and Mitigation Strategies
The robotic system operates in real-world environments and relies on multiple hardware and software components, which introduces potential risks. A structured risk assessment was conducted to identify critical risks, evaluate their likelihood and impact, and define appropriate mitigation strategies.
One of the primary risks is instability of the wireless network, which may affect communication between the robot and the external laptop. Since the system relies on TCP/IP communication for data transmission, network interruptions could lead to delayed sensor updates or temporary loss of monitoring capability. This risk is mitigated by testing the system under different network conditions and designing the dashboard to detect communication loss.
Hardware-related risks include motor failure, sensor degradation, or hardware incompatibilities. These risks can affect robot mobility and perception accuracy. Mitigation measures include early prototyping, extensive testing during development sprints, verification of component compatibility, and maintaining spare components for quick replacement.
Another technical risk is SLAM performance degradation in cluttered or dynamic environments. Inaccurate mapping or localization could impact navigation reliability. This risk is mitigated through testing in diverse indoor environments and incremental tuning of SLAM parameters.
Sensor-related risks, such as encoder slip on slippery surfaces or noise in gas sensor readings, may lead to inaccurate measurements. These risks are reduced through calibration procedures, sensor fusion techniques, and continuous monitoring of sensor data through the dashboard.
On the perception side, object detection misidentification represents a potential risk, particularly in complex visual scenes. This risk is mitigated by applying confidence thresholds to detection results and allowing human supervision through the dashboard.
Project-level risks include software integration issues, team member availability, and deadline pressure. These are mitigated through frequent integration testing, shared documentation, cross-training among team members, and clear milestone planning.
Assumptions
The system is developed based on several assumptions that define its intended operating conditions and project constraints.
Technical Assumptions
A wireless WiFi network with sufficient bandwidth (greater than 1 Mbps) is available during operation
The operating environment is structured, with clear walls and manageable levels of clutter
The LiDAR sensor maintains an unobstructed line of sight with surrounding surfaces
The camera has sufficient visual features and lighting conditions for reliable object detection
No GPS signal is required, as the system operates entirely indoors
Operational Assumptions
A trained operator is available to deploy, monitor, and supervise the system
The robot operates in areas that allow autonomous navigation
The operating environment is safe and free from immediate human hazards
Dependencies
The correct operation of the system depends on several external technologies and resources. These dependencies must be maintained to ensure system stability and consistent performance.
Key software dependencies include the ROS middleware, sensor drivers for the LiDAR, camera, IMU, encoders, and gas sensors, as well as deep learning frameworks required to run the YOLOv8 model. The system also depends on a suitable external computing device capable of performing real-time object detection.
The monitoring dashboard depends on a stable network connection and the availability of the chosen web-based framework. Data logging and system replay depend on ROS-compatible logging mechanisms.
Managing these dependencies through careful configuration, documentation, and testing is essential for maintaining system reliability and supporting future system expansion.


CHAPTER 4

CHAPTER 4: IMPLEMENTATION
4.1 Hardware Implementation
Assembly Procedure and Integration
The hardware implementation followed a systematic and phased integration approach to ensure reliability, safety, and ease of debugging. All assembly and integration activities were completed within the first four months of the project and focused on delivering a fully operational master robot.
The process combined mechanical design, electronics integration, and incremental testing, allowing early identification of issues and reducing the risk of late-stage failures<w:id w:val="-1686890194"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[17].
Phase 1: Individual Component Verification (Weeks 1–2)
The first phase focused on validating each hardware component independently before any physical assembly. This step was essential to avoid integrating faulty components into the system.
During this phase:
Each sensor (LiDAR, IMU, encoders, camera, and gas sensor) was tested individually in isolation
Custom Arduino firmware was developed and used to verify correct sensor readings
Sensor outputs were compared against expected values and known references
Calibration procedures were applied where required (e.g., encoder counts, IMU offsets)
Defective or unstable components were identified and replaced
This phase ensured that all components met functional requirements before mechanical and electrical integration.
Phase 2: Base Platform Design and Assembly (Weeks 3–4)
The second phase focused on the mechanical design and construction of the robot chassis.
Mechanical Design Process
The chassis was first modeled using Autodesk Inventor, which was used to:
Define the overall robot dimensions
Design mounting points for motors, wheels, electronics, and sensors
Verify component spacing and center of mass
Ensure mechanical stability and accessibility
After finalizing the 3D model, AutoCAD was used to produce precise 2D technical drawings. These drawings were used for fabrication and assembly, ensuring accurate hole placement, alignment, and structural consistency.
Physical Assembly
Once fabrication was completed:
The robot chassis was assembled according to the CAD designs
Front DC motors were mounted and aligned with the driven wheels
A rear caster wheel was installed to provide passive stability
Mechanical fasteners were secured, and alignment was verified
At the end of this phase, the base platform demonstrated mechanical stability and smooth manual motion.
Phase 3: Electronics Integration (Weeks 5–8)
The third phase involved integrating all electronic components and establishing reliable electrical and communication connections.
Tasks completed during this phase included:
Mounting the Raspberry Pi and Arduino Mega onto vibration-isolated mounts
Wiring motor drivers and routing high-current motor power lines
Installing sensors at positions defined during the CAD design stage
Creating organized wiring harnesses to improve modularity and maintenance
Establishing serial communication between the Raspberry Pi and Arduino
Special attention was given to separating power lines from signal lines to reduce electrical noise and interference.
Phase 4: System Testing and Debugging (Weeks 9–10)
The final phase of the hardware implementation focused on full system validation.
This phase included:
Testing motor control under software-generated commands
Verifying that all sensors provided consistent and correct data simultaneously
Validating communication between the Raspberry Pi and Arduino
Identifying and resolving integration issues related to power stability and signal integrity
Performing extended runtime tests to evaluate system robustness
By the end of this phase, the master robot operated reliably as a complete hardware system<w:id w:val="1066612477"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="F31AB12E2FBB45E08071761C148783CA"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[17].
Power System Design and Protection
The power system was identified as a critical component of the hardware implementation. Early testing revealed that inadequate power distribution could result in severe operational issues, including:
Voltage brownouts during motor startup due to high inrush current
Raspberry Pi reboots caused by 5V voltage sag
Sensor malfunction due to unstable supply voltage
Implemented Power Solution
Reliable power delivery is a critical requirement for mobile robotic systems, particularly in swarm and autonomous platforms where unstable voltage levels can lead to system resets, sensor malfunction, or permanent hardware damage. The proposed swarm robot system incorporates a dedicated, protected, and monitored power architecture designed to ensure stable operation under dynamic load conditions while maintaining hardware safety.
The system utilizes a 3S lithium-polymer (LiPo) battery with a nominal voltage of 11.1 V and a capacity of 5000 mAh, providing approximately 67 Wh of usable energy. This configuration offers an optimal balance between energy density, weight, and discharge capability, making it suitable for prolonged mobile operation without significantly increasing the robot payload.
To accommodate the heterogeneous voltage requirements of the system components, two independent buck (step-down) converters were deployed. The first converter steps the battery voltage down to 12 V, supplying sufficient and stable power to the motor drivers, which experience high current draw and rapid load variations during acceleration and obstacle avoidance. The second converter regulates the battery voltage to a stable 5 V / 3 A output, dedicated to the Raspberry Pi and other low-power electronics such as sensors and communication modules. This separation of power domains prevents motor-induced electrical noise from propagating to sensitive computational components.
To protect the system against voltage instability and transient current spikes, multiple safety mechanisms were integrated. Capacitive filtering was applied at the output stage of both buck converters to suppress high-frequency noise and smooth voltage fluctuations caused by sudden load changes. Furthermore, inline fuses were installed on the main power distribution line to safeguard against overcurrent conditions and short circuits. The fuse ratings were carefully selected based on the maximum expected system load, ensuring effective protection while avoiding nuisance tripping during normal operation.
In addition to power regulation and protection, battery voltage monitoring was implemented using an ADC-based voltage sensing circuit connected to the onboard controller. This mechanism allows continuous tracking of the battery voltage during system operation, enabling early detection of low-voltage conditions. By monitoring the battery state in real time, the system can prevent deep discharge of the LiPo battery, which is essential for maintaining battery health, ensuring operational safety, and extending overall system lifespan<w:id w:val="1223092453"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[17],<w:id w:val="722026328"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[13].
A summary of the implemented power architecture and its components is provided in Table 4.1.
Table 4.1: Power Architecture Components and Specifications
