4.2 Software Implementation
The software implementation of the autonomous robotic system represents the core operational layer that enables perception, mapping, communication, and monitoring. This implementation was carried out incrementally over the first four months of the project and closely follows the development phases documented in the project’s GitHub repository (https://github.com/GP-Golden-9/GP).
The software architecture is designed as a distributed system, where real-time robotic operations are executed on the robot, while computationally intensive tasks and visualization are handled on an external laptop. This separation was necessary due to hardware limitations and to ensure real-time performance and system stability.
All implemented software components are modular, independently testable, and integrated gradually to form a complete working system<w:id w:val="590348023"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].
4.2.1 SLAM Implementation
Development Phase and Objective
The SLAM implementation was developed to allow the robot to autonomously explore unknown indoor environments while simultaneously building a map and estimating its own position. This capability forms the spatial foundation for navigation, monitoring, and system supervision.
Implementation Description
The SLAM subsystem is implemented using a LiDAR-based 2D SLAM pipeline running on the robot. The LiDAR continuously publishes laser scan data, which is consumed by the SLAM process to generate an occupancy grid map<w:id w:val="-419555489"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[5].
The implementation process included:
Configuring the LiDAR driver to publish stable and continuous scan data
Defining coordinate frames for the robot base, LiDAR, and map
Running the SLAM algorithm while the robot moves manually and autonomously
Saving and reloading generated maps for validation
The SLAM output includes:
A 2D occupancy grid map
The robot’s estimated pose (x, y, θ)
Real-time updates during exploration
Code and Launch Integration
SLAM processes are launched through ROS-compatible launch files located in the mapping directory of the repository. These launch files initialize:
LiDAR driver
Odometry source
SLAM node
RViz visualization
Example launch command:
ros2 launch mapping slam_launch.py
RViz is used to visualize the generated map, LiDAR scans, and robot pose in real time, allowing verification of mapping accuracy.
Outcome
The implemented SLAM system successfully generates accurate indoor maps and tracks robot position during motion. This implementation is stable and forms the base for further perception and monitoring tasks<w:id w:val="-283512099"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[9].
4.2.2 Object and Human Detection Implementation
Development Phase and Objective
Object and human detection was implemented to provide visual perception and situational awareness. Due to the computational limitations of the onboard Raspberry Pi, detection was offloaded to an external laptop<w:id w:val="2062982167"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[11],<w:id w:val="-110593465"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[10].
Implementation Architecture
The detection system follows a client–server architecture over TCP/IP:
The robot captures camera frames
Frames are transmitted to the laptop
The laptop performs deep learning inference
Detection results are visualized and logged
Implementation Details
The classification directory in the repository contains multiple detection modes <w:id w:val="-2106946986"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="58FC20381F5F468D92805EF43DFA1880"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[11],<w:id w:val="1901478659"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="58FC20381F5F468D92805EF43DFA1880"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[10].
Standalone Detection
File:
classification/camera.py
This script allows direct testing of object detection using a local camera. It loads a YOLO model, captures frames, performs inference, and displays results.
This mode was used for early validation of the detection pipeline.
Network-Based Detection (Robot → Laptop)
Robot-side script:
classification/udp_rasp.py
Laptop-side script:
classification/udp_lap.py
Despite file naming, the implementation uses TCP/IP sockets.
Robot side:
Captures frames
Encodes frames
Sends frames over TCP socket
Laptop side:
Receives frames
Decodes images
Performs YOLO inference
Displays detections
Outcome
The detection system reliably identifies objects and humans in real time while maintaining robot performance. Offloading inference significantly improves system responsiveness <w:id w:val="-1417004812"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="D63276BE4BA447EDA9295EC27414B6B7"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[11],<w:id w:val="714550848"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="D63276BE4BA447EDA9295EC27414B6B7"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[10].
4.2.3 Sensor Integration
Development Phase and Objective
Sensor integration was implemented to enable the robot to perceive its environment, estimate motion, and monitor safety conditions<w:id w:val="500176217"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[12].
Sensor Architecture
Sensor integration is divided into low-level and high-level processing:
Low-Level (Arduino)
Reads encoders
Reads IMU
Reads gas sensors
Controls motors
High-Level (Raspberry Pi)
Receives sensor data via serial
Publishes data to software stack
Synchronizes sensor streams
Sensor data is then forwarded to:
Mapping subsystem
Monitoring dashboard
Logging utilities
Outcome
All sensors operate simultaneously without data loss. Sensor fusion enables accurate odometry, orientation estimation, and environmental monitoring<w:id w:val="-2022928071"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[13].
4.2.4 Robot Communication Implementation
Development Phase and Objective
The communication subsystem enables reliable data exchange between the robot and the external laptop<w:id w:val="1075326745"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[4].
Communication Architecture
The system uses TCP/IP networking for:
Video streaming
Detection data
System status communication
ROS messaging is used internally for structured data exchange.
Implementation Details
Communication includes:
Persistent TCP socket connections
Connection status monitoring
Error handling and reconnection logic
Outcome
The robot maintains stable communication with the laptop during operation, even under continuous data streaming.
4.2.5 Dashboard Implementation
Development Phase and Objective
The dashboard was implemented to provide a unified interface for monitoring, supervision, and interaction with the system<w:id w:val="612250077"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[6].

File:
dashboard/index.py
Features:

Launch:
streamlit run dashboard/index.py
2. ROS-Integrated Dashboard
File:
dashboard/dash.py
Features:
Robot status indicators

3. Full Mapping Dashboard

dashboard/dash_mapping.py

4.3 System Integration
System integration represents the phase in which all previously implemented hardware and software subsystems are combined and validated as a single, coherent autonomous robotic system. In this project, system integration was carried out incrementally during the first four months, following the successful implementation of individual hardware components, software modules, and communication mechanisms<w:id w:val="-1671637924"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[2].
The objective of this phase was to ensure that sensing, mapping, perception, communication, and monitoring modules operate simultaneously and reliably under real operating conditions. The integration process focused on stability, data consistency, and real-time performance rather than adding new functionalities.
Integration Strategy
The system integration followed a bottom-up approach, where low-level components were integrated first, followed by higher-level subsystems. This strategy reduced complexity and allowed early detection of integration conflicts.
The integration process was organized into sequential steps:
Hardware–software coupling
Sensor and actuator synchronization
Communication channel validation
Perception and mapping fusion
Dashboard and monitoring consolidation
Each step was validated independently before proceeding to the next stage.
Hardware–Software Integration
The first integration step involved coupling the physical hardware with the software stack running on the Raspberry Pi and Arduino. The Arduino was responsible for real-time motor control and sensor acquisition, while the Raspberry Pi handled higher-level processing and coordination.
Successful integration was verified by:
Executing software-generated velocity commands and observing motor response
Reading encoder, IMU, and gas sensor values through the Arduino and forwarding them to Raspberry Pi
Ensuring continuous and stable data flow without loss or delay
This stage confirmed that the hardware platform could reliably support software-controlled operation.
SLAM and Motion Integration
After hardware–software coupling, the SLAM subsystem was integrated with robot motion. During this stage, the robot was moved while LiDAR data and odometry were processed simultaneously to build a map and estimate the robot’s pose<w:id w:val="-1542982549"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/><w:sz w:val="24"/></w:rPr><w:t>[15].
Integration validation included:
Real-time map generation during robot movement
Consistent alignment between robot motion and SLAM output
Verification of coordinate frame consistency
Visualization using RViz confirmed that mapping and localization operated correctly under motion, establishing a stable navigation foundation.
Perception and Communication Integration
The next integration step involved combining visual perception with the communication subsystem. Camera frames captured on the robot were transmitted to the external laptop over a TCP/IP connection, where object and human detection was performed<w:id w:val="128530206"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[11].
This stage verified:
Reliable camera streaming without frame loss
Stable network connectivity during continuous operation
Correct synchronization between visual perception and robot state
Detection results were successfully transmitted to the monitoring system and displayed in real time, demonstrating effective integration between perception and communication subsystems.
Dashboard and Monitoring Integration
The final integration step unified all system outputs into a single monitoring interface. Data from SLAM, perception, sensors, and communication modules were aggregated and displayed through the dashboard.
This integration included:
Displaying the generated map and robot pose
Showing live camera feed with detection overlays
Presenting gas sensor readings and system status indicators
Monitoring connectivity and operational health
The dashboard successfully reflected the real-time state of the robot and provided a comprehensive overview of system operation.
Integrated System Validation
Once full integration was achieved, the system was tested under continuous operation to validate overall stability and performance. These tests confirmed that all subsystems could operate simultaneously without conflict or degradation<w:id w:val="313687581"/><w:placeholder><w:docPart w:val="DefaultPlaceholder_-1854013440"/></w:placeholder></w:sdtPr><w:sdtContent><w:r w:rsidR="00A22E46" w:rsidRPr="00A22E46"><w:rPr><w:rFonts w:ascii="Times New Roman" w:hAnsi="Times New Roman" w:cs="Times New Roman"/><w:color w:val="000000"/></w:rPr><w:t>[1].
The integrated system demonstrated:
Stable autonomous mapping
Reliable perception and detection
Continuous sensor monitoring
Robust robot-to-laptop communication
Effective real-time supervision through the dashboard
Integration Outcome
At the conclusion of the integration phase, the system functioned as a unified autonomous robotic platform. All implemented components worked together coherently, validating the design decisions made throughout the project.
The successful system integration confirms that the implemented hardware and software architecture is suitable for real-world operation and provides a solid foundation for future expansion, including multi-robot coordination in subsequent development phases.










CHAPTER 5













Chapter 5: Testing, Results, and Evaluation
5.1 Testing Strategy and Environment
The testing and evaluation phase of the Swarm Emergency Robotics System was conducted to validate the functional and non-functional requirements established during the system analysis phase. The main purpose of this chapter is to evaluate the implemented multi-robot platform under practical operating conditions and to quantify the performance of the most important system modules. The evaluation focuses on simultaneous localization and mapping (SLAM), autonomous navigation support, visual perception, environmental sensing, and distributed communication reliability.
Testing was performed after the completion of hardware assembly, software integration, and dashboard development. Therefore, the reported results reflect the behavior of the integrated prototype rather than isolated theoretical expectations. This approach is important because the performance of a robotic system is strongly affected by the interaction between mechanical motion, sensor noise, network delay, and processing limitations.
5.1.1 Testing Methodology
The evaluation methodology followed an incremental, bottom-up approach. Subsystems including sensors, mobility controls, communication interfaces, and perception modules were first evaluated in isolation to establish baseline operational metrics. These isolated tests helped confirm that each subsystem produced stable outputs before it was connected to the complete ROS 2 software stack.
After the individual tests were completed, integrated system testing was conducted to assess communication stability, shared spatial awareness, and centralized monitoring performance. This stage examined how the robot behaved when SLAM, odometry, gas sensing, video streaming, object detection, and dashboard visualization were running at the same time. The bottom-up testing sequence reduced integration risk and made it easier to identify the source of errors during the experimental phase.
5.1.2 Physical Test Environment
All experimental validations were conducted in a custom-built physical test environment measuring 4 × 4 meters. This structured indoor area was divided into four distinct rooms to simulate a constrained and partially unstructured search and rescue (SAR) scenario. The environment was selected because it allowed repeatable testing while still representing important challenges found in real indoor emergency environments.
The test environment included physical obstacles representing debris, variations in lighting conditions, narrow passages, and designated hazard zones used to trigger environmental sensors. These conditions allowed the robot to be tested against practical constraints such as limited maneuvering space, temporary visual degradation, sensor response delay, and WiFi attenuation behind obstacles.
5.1.3 Hardware and Network Setup
The testing infrastructure relied on a distributed multi-robot setup. The primary Mapping Explorer Robot (Alpha) was equipped with a Raspberry Pi 4 with 8 GB RAM, an Arduino Mega 2560, and an A1M8 LiDAR sensor, utilizing rf2o laser odometry for movement tracking. The fleet's secondary robots housed the remaining sensors: the Beta robot featured a GY-87 IMU, quadrature/hall wheel encoders, and a camera module, while the Gamma robot carried an MQ-5 gas sensor.
The Raspberry Pi executed the core processes required for SLAM support and robot-level data handling, while the Arduino handled low-level motor and sensor interfacing. An external laptop acted as the central processing hub for computationally heavy operations, especially YOLO inference and dashboard visualization. The robots and the hub communicated over a localized 5 GHz WiFi network utilizing a msgpack-over-ZMQ gateway for reliable data delivery. This configuration reflects the design decision described in previous chapters, where real-time robotic operation remains on the robot while high-load perception and supervision tasks are offloaded to a stronger processing unit.
5.2 Test Cases and Metrics
To evaluate the system objectively, specific test cases and quantitative metrics were defined before executing the experimental runs. Each test case was linked to a major system function so that the measured results could be compared against the expected behavior of the implemented architecture. The selected metrics were chosen to reflect both engineering performance and operational usefulness in search and rescue applications.
5.2.1 SLAM and Mapping Metrics
The SLAM test case required the robot to navigate the 4 x 4 meter environment at an average velocity of approximately 0.25 m/s while generating a two-dimensional occupancy grid map. The generated map was compared with the physical dimensions of the test environment to evaluate dimensional accuracy and consistency of the mapped boundaries.
The primary SLAM metric was the dimensional error between the generated occupancy grid and the measured physical environment. Additional observations included map update rate, total map generation time, and visible distortions during rotation or sharp maneuvering. These metrics indicate whether the generated map is accurate enough to support navigation and operator awareness.
5.2.2 Visual Perception Metrics
The visual perception pipeline used YOLOv8n over a TCP video stream. Because the inference process was offloaded to the external laptop, the evaluation focused on the complete perception path rather than the model alone. This included camera capture, frame transmission, inference, and visualization of detection results.
Frames Per Second (FPS): Used to measure the real-time capability of the detection pipeline under static and moving conditions.
Detection Accuracy: Used to estimate the percentage of correctly identified objects and human figures during the test cases.
Mean Average Precision (mAP): Evaluated at an Intersection over Union (IoU) threshold of 0.50 to measure the quality of the predicted bounding boxes.
The confidence threshold was adjusted according to lighting conditions to maintain a practical balance between missed detections and false positives. This was necessary because emergency environments may contain smoke, shadows, partial occlusion, and rapidly changing illumination.
5.2.3 Sensor Fusion and Localization Metrics
Localization was evaluated by programming the robot to follow a predefined 3 × 3 meter square trajectory and return to its calculated origin. This test was designed to reveal both translational drift and rotational drift after repeated turns. The complementary filter output was compared with physical ground truth measurements taken from the test environment. 
The metric used to quantify localization drift was the Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) for positional translation, defined as follows: 
RMSE = √(1/N Σᵢ₌₁ᴺ((xᵢ − x̂ᵢ)² + (yᵢ − ŷᵢ)²)) 
where (xᵢ, yᵢ) represents the physical ground truth position and (x̂ᵢ, ŷᵢ) represents the complementary filter estimated position. Rotational drift was measured in degrees by comparing the final estimated orientation with the expected orientation after completing the trajectory.
5.2.4 Gas Detection and Network Communication Metrics
Environmental sensing was evaluated by measuring the MQ-2 gas sensor response to controlled bursts of smoke and butane. The measured indicators were sensor response time, peak detected concentration, and publish latency from the sensing layer to the dashboard topic. These metrics indicate whether the system can report hazardous conditions quickly enough to support operator decision making.
Network stability was evaluated by measuring bandwidth consumption, average message latency, and packet success rate across critical ROS 2 topics during a continuous 30-minute operating window. The test included motion control messages, sensor telemetry, compressed video streaming, and SLAM map updates because these streams represent the main communication load of the system.
5.3 Experimental Results
This section presents the experimental results gathered during execution of the defined test cases. The results are organized according to the major subsystems of the robot so that each performance area can be evaluated independently before discussing the complete integrated behavior of the system.
5.3.1 SLAM and Mapping Results
The slam_toolbox algorithm with rf2o laser odometry successfully captured the geometric boundaries of the testing area and produced a usable occupancy grid for operator visualization and navigation support. The main room divisions were represented clearly, and the generated map preserved the overall structure of the 4 x 4 meter environment.
Minor distortions were observed during sharp rotational maneuvers. These distortions were mainly caused by transient odometry lag before the complementary filter stabilized the pose estimate. Despite these local errors, the resulting map remained sufficiently accurate for the scale and objectives of the prototype.

5.3.2 Visual Perception Results
By offloading YOLO inference to the external processing hub, the system maintained functional real-time perception. The average detection speed remained suitable for dashboard monitoring and operator decision support during indoor testing. 
The reduction in FPS during robot motion was attributed to motion blur in the 640x480 @ 15 fps camera feed and to the encoding and decoding overhead introduced by the msgpack-over-ZMQ transmission. Dim lighting also reduced detection confidence, which required a lower confidence threshold to maintain useful detection behavior.

5.3.3 Sensor Fusion and Localization Results
The integration of the 9-axis IMU significantly reduced rotational drift compared with using raw optical wheel odometry alone. The EKF improved heading stability during turns and helped maintain a more consistent pose estimate during short indoor trajectories.
However, translational errors remained visible over longer and more complex paths. These errors were mainly related to wheel slip, floor friction variation, and accumulated odometry uncertainty. Table 5.3 summarizes the localization results obtained during the main trajectory tests.
Table 5.3: Localization and EKF Sensor Fusion Errors

5.3.4 Environmental Sensing Results
The MQ-5 gas sensor reliably detected hazardous gas concentration changes during controlled testing. The sensing data was read by the Gamma robot's microcontroller and transmitted via an HTTP/JSON wrapper directly to the external laptop for dashboard visualization, bypassing the ROS message layer.
Figure 5.2 – Dashboard Gas Concentration Heatmap Visualization
[Insert Figure Here]
Table 5.4 presents the observed gas sensor response under clean air, simulated smoke, and butane exposure. The results show that the sensor reacted faster to butane bursts than to simulated smoke, which is consistent with the sensor characteristics and the concentration profile of the test source.
Table 5.4: MQ-5 Gas Sensor Performance

The publish latency remained low in all hazardous test cases, confirming that the environmental sensing path can support near real-time alerts on the monitoring interface.

5.3.5 Network and Communication Results
The 5 GHz wireless network handled the cumulative communication load efficiently during the 30-minute operating window. Motion commands and sensor telemetry consumed relatively low bandwidth, while the compressed video stream represented the largest portion of the total communication load.
Occasional latency spikes were observed during heavy map updates. These spikes did not critically impair teleoperation or dashboard rendering, but they indicate that network reliability remains an important factor in larger multi-robot deployments.

5.4 Analysis and Discussion
The experimental results provide empirical validation for the system architecture while also highlighting constraints that are common in academic robotic prototypes operating in physical environments. Overall, the tests confirm that the implemented system can perform indoor mapping, perception, environmental sensing, and dashboard-based monitoring in an integrated manner.
The results also show that the performance of the complete system depends on the balance between onboard computation, external processing, sensor reliability, and network stability. This balance was one of the main design considerations throughout the project.
5.4.1 System Strengths and Achievements
Evaluated as a complete cyber-physical platform, the system successfully addressed the fundamental requirements of a search and rescue robotics framework. The robot was able to map the test environment, transmit sensory and visual data, detect hazardous gas levels, and display operational information through the dashboard in real time.
Power Stability: The physical separation of power domains through dual buck converters, including 12 V for motors and 5 V/3 A for logic, successfully protected the Raspberry Pi 4 from motor-induced voltage brownouts. This stable electrical foundation allowed the ROS 2 software stack to run continuously, achieving an operational battery life of approximately 3.2 hours.
Offloaded Perception: Transporting deep learning perception to a centralized external laptop through TCP sockets proved effective. It bypassed the processing limitations of the onboard Raspberry Pi and produced an average of 20 FPS for YOLO inference while preserving onboard CPU resources for complementary filter processing and motor control timing.
Dashboard Integration: The GP Fleet Console — a PySide6 operator dashboard — successfully parsed data from the ZMQ gateway, including the SLAM map, YOLO detection results, and real-time gas telemetry. The desktop interface maintained steady visualization performance on the client laptop without blocking the main monitoring workflow.
5.4.2 Limitations and Constraints
Despite the successful results, several limitations were observed during empirical testing. These limitations do not invalidate the system design, but they identify the main technical areas that require further optimization before the platform can be extended to larger or more demanding environments. 
Odometry and Wheel Slip: Localization relies heavily on continuous wheel contact. During tests on low-friction tile surfaces, quadrature/hall encoder slip introduced measurable localization errors approaching 18.5 cm of drift over a 25-meter path. Although the IMU reduced rotational error, long operation in highly symmetrical corridors without distinct LiDAR features could still increase odometry drift. 
Network Dependencies: The operational radius of the system is strongly linked to network stability. The 5 GHz WiFi frequency was chosen for its high bandwidth capacity, especially for video streaming, but it has weaker wall penetration than lower-frequency alternatives. When the robot moved behind structural concrete corners, ZMQ message success rates dropped toward 94.8%, producing visible latency and jitter in the dashboard camera feed. 
Map Synchronization Latency: It is important to clarify that multi-robot map_server synchronization was not physically implemented in this phase. Preliminary simulated exchanges used to estimate hypothetical synchronization delay showed an average delay of 1.2 seconds. This theoretical delay is acceptable for robots operating below 0.5 m/s, but it may become a limitation for high-speed dynamic obstacle avoidance in a larger swarm deployment. 
These constraints show that the current prototype is effective for controlled indoor exploration, but future work should focus on more robust localization, stronger network planning, and improved multi-robot map synchronization.

Chapter 6: Conclusion and Future Work
6.1 Conclusion
The Swarm Robot System project successfully demonstrated an integrated approach to autonomous collaborative robotics designed for search and rescue operations. The primary goal of the project was to address the challenge of providing rapid, systematic environmental assessment without exposing human personnel to hazardous conditions. Through the implementation of a master Mapping Explorer Robot, the system achieved autonomous indoor navigation, real-time SLAM-based environmental mapping, visual object detection, and continuous environmental monitoring. 
The system architecture utilized the Robot Operating System (ROS 2) to establish a modular and distributed framework. By integrating hardware and software components effectively, the project proved the feasibility of using edge computing to offload computationally heavy tasks, such as YOLOv8n object detection, to an external processing hub while maintaining real-time control onboard the robot. 
6.2 Achievements of the Project
The project successfully accomplished several key objectives set out in the initial planning phase, proving the viability of the implemented architecture:
Hardware and Mechanical Design: The team successfully designed and constructed a stable four-wheel skid-steer mobile base.
Power Stability: A robust power architecture was implemented, featuring a 3S LiPo battery and dual independent buck converters to protect sensitive electronics, such as the Raspberry Pi 4, from motor-induced voltage brownouts.
Autonomous Mapping and Localization: The system reliably generated two-dimensional occupancy grid maps using slam_toolbox with rf2o laser odometry and a 2D LiDAR sensor.
Sensor Fusion: The integration of a complementary filter significantly improved rotational stability and reduced drift by fusing data from wheel encoders and the IMU.
Visual Perception: Real-time human and object detection was successfully implemented using YOLO models.
Offloaded Inference: By offloading the deep learning inference via TCP/IP, the system maintained an average detection speed of 20 FPS without compromising the robot's onboard control and performance.
Environmental Sensing: The integration of gas sensors allowed for the reliable detection of hazardous gases, such as simulated smoke and butane, with a rapid publish latency of less than 45 ms.
Real-Time Monitoring: The development of the GP Fleet Console — a PySide6 desktop dashboard — enabled centralized supervision, successfully parsing and aggregating data via the ZMQ gateway to display the SLAM map, YOLO detection results, and real-time gas telemetry.
6.3 Future Enhancements
While the current prototype successfully operates as an integrated cyber-physical platform, several enhancements are necessary for scaling the system and improving its robustness in more demanding environments:
Multi-Robot Coordination: The immediate next step is the full system integration of the Beta and Gamma robots to establish true swarm coordination. The system architecture is already prepared to allow the master robot (Alpha) to share its mapping and perception data with these secondary units.
Improved Localization: To address translational odometry drift caused by wheel slip on low-friction surfaces, future iterations should incorporate advanced techniques to maintain a more consistent pose estimate over longer and more complex paths.
Network Robustness: Given the system's dependency on the 5 GHz WiFi network, which experiences message success rate drops when moving behind structural corners, implementing stronger network planning or a mesh communication protocol will be vital for larger deployments.
Latency Optimization: Map synchronization latency, which averaged 1.2 seconds in preliminary simulated exchanges, needs optimization to support high-speed dynamic obstacle avoidance within a larger physical multi-robot swarm deployment.
6.4 Potential Real-World Deployment
The capabilities demonstrated by the Swarm Robot System make it highly suitable for real-world applications where human access is limited, risky, or inefficient. 
Search and Rescue (SAR): In collapsed buildings, flooded areas, or disaster zones, the system can quickly generate maps, identify victims using visual human detection, and assess structural or atmospheric hazards early in the mission. 
Industrial and Facility Monitoring: The autonomous navigation, environment mapping, and gas sensing features can be directly deployed in large operational environments such as industrial firms, warehouses, and hospitals. 
Routine Inspections: The robot can support routine inspections, safety checks, and continuous environmental monitoring tasks without disrupting daily operations or exposing personnel to danger.
